Conformal field theory from lattice fermions
Tobias J. Osborne, Alexander Stottmeister

Abstract
We provide a rigorous lattice approximation of conformal field theories given in terms of lattice fermions in 1+1-dimensions, focussing on free fermion models and Wess-Zumino-Witten models. To this end, we utilize a recently introduced operator-algebraic framework for Wilson-Kadanoff renormalization. In this setting, we prove the convergence of the approximation of the Virasoro generators by the Koo-Saleur formula. From this, we deduce the convergence of lattice approximations of conformal correlation functions to their continuum limit. In addition, we show how these results lead to explicit error estimates pertaining to the quantum simulation of conformal field theories.



1
Introduction

A rigorous mathematical formulation of quantum field theory (QFT) is one the central challenges for the new millennium
[
82
]
. A variety of approaches to build a rigorous theory of QFT have been employed throughout the past decades and have lead to deep results and insights – from
constructive QFT

[
37
,
38
,
86
]
to algebraic QFT
[
47
,
64
,
3
]
, vertex operator algebras (VOAs)
[
6
,
31
,
32
,
89
]
, and beyond
[
81
,
45
,
46
]
. However, a completely satisfactory mathematical theory of QFT is still yet to be obtained. For this reason a proof of the rigorous existence of (and mass gap for) Yang-Mills theory was selected as one of the Clay maths prize problems.

Several key difficulties to obtaining a rigorous formulation of QFT are already epitomized by
1+1
-dimensional gapless quantum field theories, in particular,
conformal field theories
(CFTs) where a variety of constructions and classifications results has been obtained
[
4
,
21
,
26
,
91
,
12
,
33
,
13
,
58
,
16
]
.

A natural strategy to prove the existence of such a theory is to somehow realize it as the limit of a sequence of
discretized approximations
(see
[
52
,
53
,
54
,
11
,
59
,
71
]
for recent attempts in a Hamiltonian setting), or
lattice models
, on finite spatial lattices
\Lambda_{N}=\varepsilon_{N}\{-L_{N},-L_{N}+1,\ldots,L_{N}-2,L_{N}-1\}\subset\varepsilon_{N}\mathbb{Z}
, where
L=\varepsilon_{N}r_{N}
is the length of the system. Associated to each lattice site
x\in\Lambda_{N}
is a quantum degree of freedom, typically modeled by a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{x}
, so that one assigns the
total Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}_{N}\subset\bigotimes_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\mathcal{H}_{x}
to the system
1
1

1

In the cases considered in this paper the total Hilbert space is provided by fermionic Fock space
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{N})
based on a one-particle space
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
for a particle with spin hopping on
\Lambda_{N}
, which can be realised as subspace of a direct sum of tensor products of smaller Hilbert spaces.
. One requires, further, a
Hamiltonian

H_{0}^{(N)}
for each discretization

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}=\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}h_{x}^{(N)},

which is a sum of self-adjoint operators
h_{x}^{(N)}
on
\mathcal{H}_{N}
with finite support centered on
x
. A key additional ingredient that must be specified is a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N}
(considered as a subalgebra of the bounded operators
B(\mathcal{H}_{N})
on
\mathcal{H}_{N}
) of basic
observables or fields
, corresponding to discretizations of their potential continuum counterparts. Given this data the task is to assign mathematical meaning to the
scaling limit
of
\{\mathfrak{A}_{N},\mathcal{H}_{N},H^{(N)}_{0}\}
.

A multitude of challenges must be overcome to realize this limit, in particular: (a) specifying the mathematical data to compare the discretizations with differing lattice size (and hence the topology for the limit); (b) identifying the correct lattice Hamiltonians for a target QFT; (c) proving the convergence of the sequence; and (d) proving that the requisite symmetry groups of the QFT are realized via projective unitary representations on the limit space. The recovery of expected continuum symmetries, i.e., requirement (d), typically poses severe difficulties (see
[
83
,
17
,
51
,
23
]
for major advances in two-dimensional critical lattice models in the Euclidean framework).

The recently introduced
operator-algebraic renormalization
(OAR)
[
85
,
69
,
11
]
supplies a novel approach to defining, and proving the existence of, the above limit in Hamiltonian approach, thus overcoming the four challenges outlined in the previous paragraph. To do this one introduces the following additional structures:

•

Refining quantum channels
, which are unital completely positive (ucp) maps,
\alpha^{N}_{N+1}:\mathfrak{A}_{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}
, connecting the observable algebras between the different scales. These induce dual
coarse-graining
trace-preserving completely positive (tpcp) maps
\mathcal{E}^{N+1}_{N}
between the state spaces, or density matrices respectively preduals,
B(\mathcal{H}_{N+1})_{*}\rightarrow B(\mathcal{H}_{N})_{*}
, in the ultraweakly continuous case. Here,
B(\mathcal{H}_{N})_{*}
denotes the predual on
B(\mathcal{H}_{N})
, i.e. the trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}_{N}
.

•

a sequence of initial (bare) states
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
on the algebras
\mathfrak{A}_{N}
, possibly given by a density matrix
\rho^{(N)}_{0}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{N})_{*}
, which are renormalized according to
\omega^{(N)}_{M}=\omega^{(N+M)}_{0}\circ\alpha^{N}_{N+M}
.

The scaling limit is then obtained via the Gelf’and-Naimark-Segal (GNS) construction
2
2

2

Informally, this means that the continuum Hilbert space and its inner product are reconstructed from the correlation functions of the basic observables
\mathfrak{A}_{N}
in the scaling limit
\omega_{\infty}
.

\{\mathcal{H}_{\infty},\pi_{\infty},\Omega_{\infty}\}
applied to the inductive limit
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{A}_{N}
and scaling-limit state
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N)}_{M}
(the existence of the latter usually requires the imposition of additional
renormalization conditions
on the sequence
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
). The convergence of Hamiltonians
H^{(N)}_{0}\rightarrow H
is considered in terms convergent operator sequences
\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|H^{(N)}_{0}-\alpha^{M}_{N}(H^{(M)}_{0})\|_{*}
in a suitable operator topology (or their associated unitary respectively automorphism groups)
[
22
]
. It is important to note that this convergence is a direct extension of the convergence that describes the inductive-limit algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
, i.e., each element
O\in\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
is the limit of a convergent sequence
O_{N}
in the sense that:

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|O_{N}-\alpha^{M}_{N}(O_{M})\|_{C^{*}}

\displaystyle=0.

To obtain a (full) CFT via an OAR scaling limit one needs to realize the infinite-dimensional conformal group of symmetries via projective unitary representations on the limit space. This requires the additional identification of a discretized family of generators corresponding to the
Virasoro algebra with central charge

c

[
21
]
,

\displaystyle[L_{k},L_{k’}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)L_{k+k’}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{c}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}-1),

\displaystyle [\overline{L}_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)\overline{L}_{k+k’}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{c}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}-1),

\displaystyle[L_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}]

\displaystyle=0.

Such a family is furnished by the
Koo-Saleur (KS) approximants

[
60
]

\displaystyle L_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{k}^{(N)}\!+\!\tfrac{\pi\varepsilon_{N}}{2L\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}\Big[H_{k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big]\Big),

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{k}^{(N)}\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{-k}^{(N)}\!+\!\tfrac{\pi\varepsilon_{N}}{2L\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}\Big[H_{-k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big]\Big),

where the
lattice hamiltonian Fourier modes
are given by

\displaystyle H_{k}^{(N)}=\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{ikx}h^{(N)}_{x},

with
k\in\Gamma_{N}
in the dual lattice. The inductive family of ucp maps
\alpha_{N+1}^{N}
must be carefully chosen to ensure the existence of (meaningful) scaling limits (interestingly the inductive-limit algebra is somewhat insensitive to the specific realization of each map
[
5
]
). We focus on two classes (and their combination): a real-space renormalisation group based on wavelets and their scaling functions
[
20
]
which explicitly maintains locality of the algebra and a momentum-space renormalisation group based on a sharp cutoff in momentum space. With this preliminary data in hand we turn now to a summary of the main results.

1.1
Main results

Let us give a brief overview of the results presented in this paper. The main results are presented in abridged form as Theorems
A
to
C
accompanied by references to the appropriate statements in the main text. The basis of our results is a scaling limit construction for free lattice fermions using OAR. This entails that our approximation results for CFTs, i.e. the convergence of the KS approximants and other current-type fields, are currently restricted to free-fermion CFTs, products thereof and certain CFTs embeddable into those (see below). Although, we phrase all results in terms of complex fermion algebras, fully analogous statements also hold for suitably defined self-dual fermion algebras (Majorana fermions), as explained in the main sections. Specifically, we consider scaling limits
\omega_{\infty}
of the ground state
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
of a quadratic lattice Dirac Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\left(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(2)}_{x}-a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}+\textup{h.c.}+\lambda_{N}\left(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(1)}_{x}-a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}\right)\right),

in the massless case
\lambda_{N}=0
. Here,
a_{x},a^{{\dagger}}_{x}
are the usual annihilation and creation operators generating a complex fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N}
associated with the lattice
\Lambda_{N}
.

The first main result of the paper concerns the convergence of the KS approximants to the continuum Virasoro generators in projective unitary positive-energy representations with central charge
c=\tfrac{1}{2},1
associated with free-fermion scaling limits
\omega_{\infty}
. The convergence holds on the natural domain of finite-energy vectors in Fock space (with respect to the chiral conformal Hamiltonian
L_{\pm,0}
arising from
H^{(N)}_{0}
)
[
15
]
. Here and in the following,
\pm
denotes the chiral and anti-chiral components defined in Section
2.4
. In the main text, this result is stated as Theorem
4.11
, where all details can be found.

Theorem A

(Convergence of the Koo-Saleur approximants for
\pi_{\infty}
)
.

Let
\omega_{\infty}
be a massless scaling limit of the free-fermion ground state
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
and
\pi_{\infty}
its GNS representation. The chiral Koo-Saleur approximants,
L^{(N)}_{k,\pm}
, converge strongly to the continuum Virasoro generators,
L_{k,\pm}
, on the dense, common core
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{fin}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
spanned by finite-energy vectors of the chiral conformal Hamiltonian
L_{\pm,0}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|(:\!\pi_{\infty}(\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm,k})\!)\!:-L_{\pm,k})\phi\|

\displaystyle=0,

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}_{\textup{fin}}
.

The proof of this theorem is a consequence of the basic Lemma
4.10
and exploits the momentum-cutoff renormalization group given in Definition
3.9
in combination with known results concerning the implementability of Bogoliubov transformations in quasi-free representations of fermion algebras (see Section
2.1.1
).

A version of this result for the Fock representation with
c=0
(not of positive energy) is given in Theorem
4.6
and another one for smeared Virasoro generators in Theorem
4.16
. Moreover, a similar result can be obtained for the lattice approximation of Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) currents (see Section
5
and Theorem
5.1
).

The second major result of our paper concerns correlation functions of fermion operators under the action of conformal transformations generated by Virasoro generators. Informally it states that the finite-scale dynamical correlation function approximate the continuum dynamical correlation functions for arbitrary collections of operators in the (chiral) fermion algebra (especially including trace-class generators of quasi-free derivations as in
[
94
]
). This results is referred to as Theorem
6.1
together with Corollary
6.2
in the main text.

Theorem B

(Convergence of fermion correlation functions)
.

Let
s\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
be a sufficiently regular, compactly supported orthonormal Daubechies scaling function, and
\pi_{\infty}
be the scaling limit representation of the fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
associated with the free-fermion scaling limit
\omega_{\infty}
. Then, for any convergent sequences
\{A_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
\{B_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
with limits
A,B\in\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
and uniformly in
t\in\mathbb{R}
on compact intervals, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\Omega^{(N)}_{0},\pi^{(N)}(A_{N})\sigma^{(N)}_{t}(\pi^{(N)}(B_{N}))\Omega^{(N)}_{0})

\displaystyle=(\Omega_{\infty},\pi_{\infty}(A)\sigma_{t}(\pi_{\infty}(B))\Omega_{\infty})\;,

where
\Omega^{(N)}_{0}
,
\pi^{(N)}
are the GNS-vector and -representation of
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
.
\sigma^{(N)}_{t}
and
\sigma_{t}
are
1
-parameter (semi-)groups of Bogoliubov transformations generated by a (smeared) Koo-Saleur approximant or a (non-)abelian current and their continuum analogues respectively.

The proof of this theorem is implied the existence of the scaling limit of ground states due to Lemma
3.11
and Lemmata
4.5
,
4.10
&
4.15
because of the semi-group convergence theorem
[
55
, Theorem 2.16, p. 504]
, cf. also
[
24
, Theorem 1.8, p. 141]
.

The final major result pertains to the correlation functions of the Virasoro algebra in free-fermion CFTs yielding a result comparable to that presented in
[
96
]
. Similar to Theorem
B
, we find that the correlation functions of smeared Virasoro generators respectively their exponentials, which generate local Virasoro nets
[
12
,
33
]
(here with central charge
c=\tfrac{1}{2},1
), are obtained as limits of ground state correlation functions of the Koo-Saleur approximants. This results is referred to as Theorem
6.3
in the main text.

Theorem C

(Convergence of Virasoro correlation functions)
.

Let
s\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
be a sufficiently regular, compactly supported orthonormal Daubechies scaling function, and
\pi_{\infty}
be the scaling limit representation of the fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
associated with the free-fermion scaling limit
\omega_{\infty}
. Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and convergent sequences of smearing functions
X_{N,p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\rightarrow\infty}}{{\rightarrow}}X_{p}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
p=1,...,n
with sufficient regularity, we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}:\!(\pi_{\infty}\circ\alpha^{N}_{\infty})(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p}))\!:\Omega_{\infty})

\displaystyle=(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}L_{\pm}(X_{p})\Omega_{\infty})\;,

and similarly,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{i:(\pi_{\infty}\circ\alpha^{N}_{\infty})(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})):}\Omega_{\infty})

\displaystyle=(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{iL_{\pm}(X_{p})}\Omega_{\infty})\;,

Moreover, the finite-scale correlation functions of the scaling limit state
\omega_{\infty}
can be approximated in terms of the renormalized finite-scale states:

\displaystyle(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}:\!\pi_{\infty}(\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)\!)\!:\Omega_{\infty})=\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}(\prod_{p=1}^{n}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})-\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)\!)\!)

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N)}_{M}(\prod_{p=1}^{n}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})-\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)\!)\!)

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N+M)}_{0}\!(\prod_{p=1}^{n}(\alpha^{N}_{N+M}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}\!(X_{N,p})\!)\!-\!\omega^{(N+M)}_{0}\!(\alpha^{N}_{N+M}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}\!(X_{N,p})\!)\!)\!)\!)\;,

and similarly,

\displaystyle(\Omega_{\infty},\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{i:\pi_{\infty}(\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)\!):}\Omega_{\infty})=\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}(\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{iL^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})-i\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)})

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N)}_{M}(\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{iL^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})-i\omega^{(N)}_{M}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)})

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N+M)}_{0}\!(\prod_{p=1}^{n}e^{i\alpha^{N}_{N+M}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)-i\omega^{(N+M)}_{0}\!(\alpha^{N}_{N+M}(L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N,p})\!)\!)})\;,

where
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}=\omega_{\infty}\circ\alpha^{N}_{\infty}
, and
L^{(N)}_{\pm}(X_{N})\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N}}\!\hat{X}_{N|k}L^{(N)}_{\pm,k}
(similarly for
L_{\pm}(X)
).

The proof of this theorem is a consequence of the convergence of the scaling limit procedure for the ground states stated in Lemma
3.11
in combination with the extension of Theorem
A
to smeared Koo-Saleur approximants and Virasoro generators given in Theorem
4.16
. It equally well applies to WZW currents instead because of Theorem
5.1
and its generalization.

In view of the seminal work of Koo and Saleur
[
60
]
, it should be noted that the convergence of the KS approximants for free-fermion CFTs was anticipated therein. Specifically, exact agreement with the Virasoro generators in expectation values with respect to lattice ground states in a formal scaling limit construction was found for central charges
c=0,\tfrac{1}{2},-2
. Thus, our results yield a rigorous justification of the computations in
[
60
]
for the cases
c=0,\tfrac{1}{2}
. Moreover, our construction of scaling limits
\omega_{\infty}
using OAR provides a detailed mathematical framework for the scaling limit considered by Koo and Saleur, allowing for the approximation of Virasoro generators in an operator sense (strong operator topology) instead of expectation values (weak operator topology). This is achieved by explicitly constructing a Hilbert space of states via the GNS representation of
\omega_{\infty}
corresponding to scaling limits of the low-lying excited states relative to the lattice ground state
\omega^{(N)}_{0}
. Interestingly, the exact formal as well as rigorous results for the approximation of the Virasoro generators by the Koo-Saleur formula essentially rely on the definition of the correct scaling limit representation by subtracting vacuum/ground-state expectation values, which corresponds to normal ordering as the Virasoro generators and KS approximants are quadratic expression in free fermions. Therefore, it appears reasonable to assume that the failure of the exact computations in
[
60
]
for general central charge
c\leq 1
, is due, at least in part, to the insufficiency of this procedure in the general case. To allow for a more direct comparison between
[
60
]
, we translate our formulation in terms of fermions into the language of Temperley-Lieb algebras
[
88
,
44
]
in Section
4.2.1
.

We point out that in addition to the treatment of currents of WZW models, our results also cover the scaling limit of the transverse-field Ising model, which is intimately related to the two-dimensional classical Ising model
[
65
,
79
]
, in the following sense: (1) the lattice ground states converge to their appropriate limit on the subalgebra of even observables (by the Jordan-Wigner isomorphism
[
26
]
), (2) the Koo-Saleur approximants converge to the correct Virasoro generators in the representation associated with the scaling limit (as also observed in
[
96
]
). The convergence with respect to the full observable algebra including odd observables
[
63
,
7
]
and their conformal covariance requires additional work going beyond the scope of the present paper and will be treated in a separate publication adapting results from
[
78
]
.

Although, in this paper, we restricted our treatment to scaling limits of fermion systems and their quasi-free representations, or systems based thereon such as WZW models, not least to show the validity of our approach to recover conformal symmetry in the scaling limit via OAR in the clearest possible manner, the general method is not restricted to this setting. But, to handle, for example, arbitrary rational CFTs, it will be necessary to address lattice models involving anyonic chains
[
28
,
96
]
. To employ OAR to such models, we need an appropriate family of refining maps. Such a potential family of such maps is induced by the Jones-Wenzl projection
[
92
,
56
]
. However, this it is beyond the scope of this paper and will be presented elsewhere.

Furthermore, our current treatment of WZW models only allows for a central charge in the range
r\leq c\leq D
, where
D
is the number of copies of fermions and
r
is the rank of Lie algebra of the model. To allow for a central charge
c<1
, a natural next step is to check the compatibility with the coset construction
[
39
,
40
]

In addition, it would be interesting to understand whether the analysis presented here extends to the setting of so-called “symplectic fermions”
[
77
,
57
,
34
]
with
c=-2
corresponding to one of the case where exact formal results were obtained by Koo and Saleur
[
60
]
. Such an extension appears to be feasible because the algebraic structure of the continuum chiral algebra generated by the symplectic two-component fermion can be discretized by multi-scale decomposition using the adjoint conditional expectations
\alpha^{M}_{N}
,
M>N
, associated with the refining quantum channels (cp. Section
1.2
). But, it needs to be clarified whether such a discretization of the continuum model can be recovered via the renormalization group flow in OAR from a suitable Hamiltonian lattice model, e.g. the XX spin chain formulation of dense polymers
[
77
]
. This said, we leave a detailed analysis of this case for future work.

1.2
Comparison with other approaches

The strategy outlined above is by no means the only one may adopt to realize QFTs rigorously. Indeed, tremendous effort and major successes have been obtained by focussing on other approaches where the Hilbert space of the quantum field theory is realized directly in the continuum or by probabilistic Euclidean methods (see
[
86
]
for an overview), and most recently in the context of stochastic quantization (see, e.g.,
[
46
,
49
]
). Our approach can be viewed as closely related to the quantum mechanical constructions of Glimm-Jaffe and others
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
in contrast with Euclidean approaches. Moreover, considerable dividends are paid by the discretization approach because, if realized correctly, one obtains a sequence of quantum lattice systems which are directly amenable to
quantum simulation
on quantum computers (we comment on this aspect in Remark
4.20
, see also
[
72
]
). This was emphasized by Zini and Wang, who made the first concerted effort to realize CFTs via limits of Hamiltonian lattice models. Below we summarize their approach to scaling limits in the context of OAR.

Zini and Wang
[
96
]
define a
low-energy scaling limit
with the following data:

•

A nested sequence of (energy-bounded) subspaces
\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}\subset\mathcal{H}^{E^{\prime}}_{N}
, associated with energy scales
E^{\prime}\geq E\geq 0
,

•

connecting unitiaries

\phi^{E}_{N}:\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N+1}
for sufficiently large
N
, i.e. a stabilizing dimension
\dim\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}=d_{E}
for all
N>>0
,

•

and the extension property:
\phi^{E}_{N}=\phi^{E^{\prime}}_{|\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}}
for
E^{\prime}\geq E
.

The scaling limit results from an inductive limit
\mathcal{H}^{E}_{\infty}=\varinjlim_{N}\mathcal{H}^{E}_{N}
along the connecting unitaries, and, by the extension property, gives a coherent system of energy-bounded Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{E}_{\infty}\subset\mathcal{H}^{E^{\prime}}_{\infty}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
for
E\leq E^{\prime}
. The convergence of Hamiltonians is understood in terms of convergent operator sequences
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{N^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty}\|H^{(N^{\prime})}_{0|E}-\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}}H^{(N)}_{0|E}(\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}})^{*}\|_{*}
, for some suitable semi-norm
\|\!\ .\!\ \|_{*}
, which should determine
H_{|E}
(e.g. by the second Trotter-Kato approximation theorem
[
73
]
). Here,
|E
indicates the restriction of the Hamiltonians to energies below
E
, and
\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}}
results from the iteration of the connecting unitaries between scales
N\leq N^{\prime}
. Again, because of coherence in
E
, this defines
H
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
.

Zini and Wang also define the notion of
strong scaling limit
, which describes the case, when
\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}}
results from the restriction of an isometry
\phi_{N}:\mathcal{H}_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{N+1}
to the energy-bounded subspace.

For
Q_{N}(\!\ .\!\ ,\!\ .\!\ )=\langle\!\ .\!\ ,O_{N}\!\ .\!\ \rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{N}}
, the sesquilinear form associated with
O_{N}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N}
, convergence is defined as convergence,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{N^{\prime}\rightarrow}Q_{N^{\prime}}(\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}}(\!\ .\!\ ),\phi^{E}_{N\rightarrow N^{\prime}}(\!\ .\!\ ))

\displaystyle=Q^{E},

on
\mathcal{H}^{E}_{\infty}
(similar to the convergence of the Hamiltonians).

Zini and Wang observe
[
96
, p. 898]
that compatible embeddings
\tau_{N}:\mathfrak{A}_{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{N}\circ O_{N}

\displaystyle=\tau_{N}(O_{N}) \circ\phi_{N},

in the special case of a strong scaling limit, yield an (operator-)algebraic scaling limit of the observable algebras
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{A}_{N}
along these embeddings. They also conjecture that higher-level anyonic chains have this property.

We now explain how these scaling limits are recovered via OAR: Assuming that we have constructed the inductive limit algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
, together with a scaling limit state
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}
, we can perform the GNS construction at each scale
N
resulting in a sequence of triples
\{\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{(N)},\pi_{\infty}^{(N)},\Omega_{\infty}^{(N)}\}
together with isometries
V^{N}_{N+1}:\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{(N)}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{(N+1)}
induced by
\alpha^{N}_{N+1}
such that
V^{N}_{N+1}\Omega_{\infty}^{(N)}=\Omega_{\infty}^{(N+1)}
and:

\displaystyle V^{N}_{N+1}\circ\pi_{\infty}^{(N)}(O_{N})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\infty}^{(N+1)}(\alpha^{N}_{N+1}(O_{N}))\circ V^{N}_{N+1}.

Thus, we find that the compatibility condition of Zini and Wang is precisely recovered by the inductive-limit structure of the scaling-limit Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
in the operator-algebraic formulation. Moreover, if the GNS representations
\{\mathcal{H}_{M}^{(N)},\pi_{M}^{(N)},\Omega_{M}^{(N)}\}
of the finitely renormalized states
\omega^{(N)}_{M}
are unitarily equivalent, e.g. assuming a Stone-von Neumann-type result at finite scales, that is, if there are (connecting) unitaries,

\displaystyle\phi^{N}_{M}:\mathcal{H}_{M}^{(N)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{M+1}^{(N)},

we can recover the structure of a low-energy scaling limit (the extension property follows again from the GNS construction because
\omega^{(N)}_{M+1}=\omega^{(N+1)}_{M}\circ\alpha^{N}_{N+1}
), such that
N
plays the role of the energy scale
E
. The overall structure is illustrated in Figure
1
.

\omega^{(N)}_{0}
\omega^{(N)}_{1}
\omega^{(N)}_{2}
\dots
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}
\mathfrak{A}_{N}
\phi^{N}_{0}
\phi^{N}_{1}
\phi^{N}_{2}
\omega^{(N+1)}_{0}
\omega^{(N+1)}_{1}
\dots
\omega^{(N+1)}_{\infty}
\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}
\phi^{N+1}_{0}
\phi^{N+1}_{1}
\mathcal{E}^{N+1}_{N}
\mathcal{E}^{N+1}_{N}
\alpha^{N}_{N+1}
\omega^{(N+2)}_{0}
\dots
\omega^{(N+2)}_{\infty}
\mathfrak{A}_{N+2}
\phi^{N+2}_{0}
\mathcal{E}^{N+2}_{N+1}
\alpha^{N+1}_{N+2}
\iddots
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 1
:
Enriched version of Wilson’s triangle of renormalization
[
93
]
: Coarse-graining quantum channels (
\mathcal{E}
’s) between quantum states, refining quantum channels between observables/fields (
\alpha
’s), and low-energy unitaries (
\phi
’s) between renormalized representations (if existent).

(Such structures are also achieved in the operator-algebraic renormalization of a scalar lattice field
[
69
]
.)

It is worth pointing out that such a structure is not immediately available for the operator-algebraic renormalization of (free) massless chiral lattice fermions in 1+1-dimensions considered here, where the finitely renormalized states
\omega^{(N)}_{M}
are not pure (due to the entanglement of the chiral halves) in contrast with their scaling limit (given by the Hardy projection).

An important difference of our approach with the construction by Zini and Wang is that our renormalized Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{M}^{(N)}
and the connecting unitaries
\phi^{N}_{M}
are not necessarily associated with energy bounds, and indeed can be far more general, e.g., in the wavelet approach formulated in
[
85
]
and in Section
3.1
the parameter
N
is associated with a spatial resolution, while energy bounds are similar to the momentum-cutoff approach, see
[
69
]
and Section
3.2
. This flexibility enables us to overcome a major challenge facing the construction of Zini and Wang’s strong scaling limit, namely that the addition of irrelevant terms to the lattice discretizations, resulting in lattice-scale artifacts, makes it difficult if not impossible to find connecting unitaries.

In summary, the core differences between the scaling limit construction of Zini and Wang, and the OAR, may be summarized as follows:

•

In the framework of Zini and Wang, the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
of the scaling limit, in other words the sector of the observables, is fixed from the outset by the specification of the connecting unitaries (
\phi
’s) and the energy-bounded subspaces, or by the connecting isometries in the strong scaling limit. Then, the renormalization of the Hamiltonian and other observables amounts to determining all compatible operators (or sesquilinear forms), i.e.
\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}
, on
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
. Because the sector is fixed by the provision of the connecting unitaries, quantum states are in essence not renormalized.

•

In the framework of operator-algebraic renormalization, we fix a minimal set of observables over all scales, i.e.
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
, the consistency of which is described by the refining quantum channels (
\alpha
’s). In turn, the renormalization of states is used to find (all) sensible, compatible states on
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty}
by starting from initial choices on each scale.

In this respect, we observe that the two notions of convergence of Hamiltonians (and observables) relative to the scaling limit
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
can be compared using the GNS isometries, because

\displaystyle V^{N}_{N+1}\circ\pi_{\infty}^{(N)}(O_{N})(V^{N}_{N+1})^{*}

\displaystyle\!=\!\pi_{\infty}^{(N+1)}(\alpha^{N}_{N+1}(O_{N}))p_{\im V^{N}_{N+1}}

where
p_{\im V^{N}_{N+1}}
is projection onto the image of
V^{N}_{N+1}
.

A second key contribution to the mathematical literature on approximations of CFTs is found in papers building tensor-network approximations via matrix product states
[
61
,
62
]
in the context of VOAs or wavelets and their scaling functions in a Hamiltonian lattice setup
[
27
,
48
,
94
]
. Specifically, in the latter group of papers a central role is also played by an inductive system of quantum channels
\alpha^{N}_{M}
,
M>N
, or more precisely their adjoint conditional expectations
[
25
]
,

\displaystyle\alpha^{M}_{N}:\mathfrak{A}_{M}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle>N,

These are used to define coarse-grained fermion fields as well as finite-scale approximants of (trace-class) second-quantized one-particle operators. In that respect, it should be noted that the main theorem of
[
94
]
concerning the approximation of correlation functions of the continuum free fermion field only applies to the insertion of basic fermions in the sense of our Theorem
B
, see
[
94
, Theorem 4.4, p. 31]
). Thus, more general operators such as Virasoro generators
L_{\pm,k}
or WZW currents are explicitly excluded. Such restriction do not apply in the OAR approach we adopt here. Another important difference in comparison with the operator-algebraic approach is the need for a continuum model to define the approximants using the conditional expectations
\alpha^{\infty}_{N}
, which is not intrinsically required by our method. It should also be noted that the approximation of correlation functions achieved in
[
48
,
94
]
is deduced from an error bound of the form,

\displaystyle|\langle O_{1}\ldots O_{n}\rangle^{(N)}\!-\!\langle O_{1}...O_{n}\rangle_{\text{cont}}|\!\lesssim\!\mathcal{O}(2^{cN})\!+\!\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\log\varepsilon),

requiring an
\varepsilon
-dependent choice of scaling functions to define the lattice correlation function
\langle O_{1}\dots O_{n}\rangle^{(N)}
,
N=N(\varepsilon)
, and achieve a desired degree of accuracy prohibiting a rigorous proof of convergence in the scaling limit. Although it should be said, that
N
is expected to decay exponentially with
\varepsilon
, which would entail the aforesaid convergence, according to
[
94
]
and the references therein. Such an adaptation of the chosen scaling functions to the accuracy goal of the approximation is not required by OAR.

Finally, let us comment on further results in the literature.

In
[
43
]
(see also
[
42
]
), there is very interesting work on the KS approximants in the setting of the XXZ spin chain in terms of Temperley-Lieb algebras as in
[
60
]
. The authors consider a certain “weak-scaling” procedure, which we would paraphrase as weak operator convergence of KS approximants and products thereof. Interestingly, the weak convergence is restricted to a certain class of scaling states lattice states, which we believe roughly correspond to the states identified by the GNS representation of the scaling limit
\{\mathfrak{A}_{\infty},\omega_{\infty}\}
in OAR. Moreover,
[
43
]
provides a wealth of conjectures, backed by extensive numerics, concerning the limits appearing in the “weak-scaling” procedure, and, thus, it would be worthwhile to investigate whether our methods allow for a proof at least further progress, potentially exploiting the connection with the formulation based on Temperley-Lieb algebras for general
c\neq 0
(see Section
4.2.1
). In particular, it would be interesting whether our methods allow to lift the “weak-scaling” procedure to a “strong” version (in the sense of operator topologies).

As mentioned above, there is an ongoing concerted and very successful effort in the Euclidean setting using classical probabilistic methods and, specifically, the concept of
discrete holomorphicity
(see
[
51
,
19
,
18
]
) and references therein. In
[
51
]
it is shown how to explicitly relate discrete holomorphic structures with the continuum Virasoro algebra, thereby establishing a correspondence between correlation functions of lattice local fields and their counterparts in the continuum. As this is analogous to various degrees to what is achieved in the work presented here and the other Hamiltonian frameworks discussed above, it would be interesting to explore potential connections. Concretely, such an analysis could start from the ideas presented
[
29
]
and by exploiting the natural connection between Euclidean and Hamiltonian formulation of critical systems via the transfer matrix formalism.

1.3
Outline of the article

In this paper we develop the theory of OAR and apply it to the discrete approximation of conformal symmetries in continuum CFTs. This yields an explicit family of lattice systems and bounds which may be directly exploited to carry out the quantum simulation of CFTs on quantum computers (see our companion paper
[
72
]
).

The structure of the paper is as follows: In Section
2
, we provide a detailed overview of known results on complex and self-dual fermion algebras required for the proofs of our results. Moreover, we introduce the necessary setup for fermions on lattices required for our scaling limit construction. In Section
3
, we introduce the scaling maps required for OAR: (1) the wavelet renormalization group, (2) the momentum-cutoff renormalization group. In addition, we discuss the compatibility of the wavelet renormalization group with the quasi-local structure of the fermion algebra in the scaling limit, give a basic decay estimate for wavelet approximations, and prove the convergence of the scaling limit of the ground states of the lattice Dirac Hamiltonian. In Section
4
, we discuss Koo-Saleur approximants and their formal scaling limit yielding the Virasoro generators, before we prove their convergence in the scaling limit as alluded to in Theorem
A
. In Section
5
, we explain the modifications necessary to apply the convergence results to cover WZW currents. Finally, in Section
6
, we apply the convergence results for the KS approximants to deduce the convergence of (dynamical) correlation functions of various kinds as exemplified by Theorems
B
&
C
.

2
Lattice fermions in 1+1-dimensions

2.1
The algebra of canonical anti-commutation relations

Let us recall some basic structures and results concerning the canonical anti-commutation relations (CAR). For further details we refer to the general references
[
26
,
9
]
.

The complex CAR algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})
of a complex Hilbert space
\mathfrak{h}
, the
one-particle space
, is the universal unital
C^{*}
-algebra generated by anti-linear map,

\displaystyle a:\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}),

(1)

referred to as
annihilation operators
and subject to,

\displaystyle\{a(\xi),a^{{\dagger}}(\eta)\}

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\{a(\xi),a(\eta)\}

\displaystyle=0=\{a^{{\dagger}}(\xi),a^{{\dagger}}(\eta)\},

\displaystyle\xi,\eta

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h},

(2)

where
a^{{\dagger}}(\eta)=a(\eta)^{*}
denotes the adjoint
creation operator
. Each
a(\xi)
is automatically bounded:
\|a(\xi)\|=\|\xi\|_{\mathfrak{h}}
. We will also refer to the maps
a,a^{{\dagger}}
as a
complex fermion
. We also recall that there is a
*
-isomorphism
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})\cong\otimes_{n=1}^{\dim\mathfrak{h}}M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
which we come back to in Section
2.7
.

The irreducible standard
Fock representation
of
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})
on the anti-symmetric Fock space
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h})=\oplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\wedge^{n}\mathfrak{h}
, with vacuum vector
\Omega_{0}
, is given by:

\displaystyle a^{{\dagger}}(\xi)\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n}

\displaystyle=\xi\wedge\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n},

\displaystyle\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n}

\displaystyle\in\wedge^{n}\mathfrak{h},

(3)

where
\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n}=(n!)^{\frac{1}{2}}S_{-}(\eta_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\eta_{n})
with the projection,
S_{-}:\mathfrak{h}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\wedge^{n}\mathfrak{h}
, on the anti-symmetric subspace. Subsequently, we call
P_{\leq n}
the projection onto the subspace
\oplus_{m=0}^{n}\wedge^{m}\mathfrak{h}
, and
P_{n}=P_{\leq n}-P_{\leq n-1}
. On
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h})
, we denote by
(-1)^{F}
the
parity operator
, i.e. the unitary operator implementing the grading of
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})
defined by:
\alpha_{-1}(a(\xi))=a(-\xi)=-a(\xi)
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}
. We remark that
(-1)^{F}\notin\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{H})
unless
\dim\mathfrak{h}<\infty
.

To treat Majorana fermions, we need in addition to
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}
the notion a
self-dual CAR algebra

\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)
for some (charge) conjugation
C:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}
. The
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{H},C)
is generated by an anti-linear map,

\displaystyle\Psi:\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C),

(4)

which we refer to as the
Majorana fermion or operator
, subject to,

\displaystyle\{\Psi(\xi),\Psi(\eta)\}

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,C\eta\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\Psi(\xi)^{*}

\displaystyle=\Psi(C\xi),

\displaystyle\xi,\eta

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}.

(5)

We note that complex and Majorana fermions can be related by a projection,
P:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}
, such that
CP=(1-P)C
(called a
basis projection
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)

\displaystyle\cong\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(P\mathfrak{h}),

\displaystyle a(\xi)

\displaystyle=\Psi(\xi),

\displaystyle a^{{\dagger}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\Psi(C\xi),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in P\mathfrak{h}.

(6)

For the description of ground states of quadratic Hamiltonians, we need the notion of a quasi-free states
\omega
on
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})
respectively
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)
(gauge invariant in the first case). In both cases, the state
\omega
is completely determined by its two-point function, i.e.
\omega(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta))
respectively
\omega(\Psi(\xi)\Psi(\eta)^{*})
.

In the first case, the two-point function defines a positive operator,
0\leq S\leq 1
, such that:

\displaystyle\omega_{S}(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,(1-S)\eta\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\xi,\eta

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h},

(7)

\displaystyle \omega_{S}(a(\xi_{1})\dots a(\xi_{n})a^{{\dagger}}(\eta_{n+1})\dots a^{{\dagger}}(\eta_{n+m}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{n,m}\det((\langle\xi_{i},(1-S)\eta_{j}\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}})_{i,j=1}^{n}).

Clearly, the vacuum vector
\Omega_{0}
corresponds to
S=0
. Moreover,
\omega_{S}
is a pure state if and only if
S
is a projection.

In the second case, the two-point function also defines a positive operator,
0\leq S\leq 1
, with the additional property
CS=(1-S)C
, such that:

\displaystyle\omega_{S}(\Psi(\xi)\Psi(\eta)^{*})

\displaystyle=\langle\xi,(1-S)\eta\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}},

\displaystyle\xi,\eta

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h},

(8)

\displaystyle \omega_{S}(\Psi(\xi_{1})\dots\Psi(\xi_{n}))

\displaystyle=\pf((\langle\xi_{i},(1-S)C\xi_{j}\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}})_{i,j=1}^{n}),

where
\pf
denotes the Pfaffian, which we define to vanish for
n
odd. As before, the state
\omega_{S}
is pure if and only if
S
is a (basis) projection.

In both cases, the Gelfand-Naimark-Segal (GNS) representation of pure states,
S^{2}=S
, can be realized on anti-symmetric Fock space by:

\displaystyle\pi_{S}(a(\xi))

\displaystyle=a((1-S)\xi)+a^{{\dagger}}(JS\xi),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h},

(9)

for some conjugation
J:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}
with
JS=SJ
, respectively,

\displaystyle\pi_{S}(\Psi(\xi))

\displaystyle=a((1-S)\xi)+a^{{\dagger}}(CS\xi),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}.

(10)

Clearly, the notation is consistent with
\pi_{0}
being the Fock representation (
3
). In the non-pure situation, an analogous realization on the doubled Fock space,
\mathfrak{F}_{a}(\mathfrak{h}^{\oplus 2})=\mathfrak{F}_{a}(\mathfrak{h})^{\otimes 2}
, via the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-twisted tensor product,
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})^{\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}2}=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}^{\oplus 2})
respectively
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)^{\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}2}=\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h}^{\oplus 2},C\oplus(-C))
, is possible because,

\displaystyle P_{S}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}S&S^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-S)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
S^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-S)^{\frac{1}{2}}&1-S\end{pmatrix},

(11)

is a (basis) projection
[
2
,
66
]
.

2.1.1
Implementation of Bogoliubov transformations

Central to our analysis of the recovery of conformal symmetry via lattice approximations is the question of implementability of
Bogoliubov or quasi-free transformations
of the complex and Majorana fermion algebras,
\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h})
respectively
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)
, in quasi-free representations (
9
) & (
10
). To this end, we briefly recall some well-known formulas and results and refer to
[
2
,
66
,
76
,
30
,
14
,
26
]
for further details.

In its basic form a Bogoliubov transformation
\alpha_{T}
is densely defined morphism of the CAR associated with an invertible, bounded operator,
T\in B(\mathfrak{h})
, on the one-particle space:

\displaystyle\alpha_{T}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi))

\displaystyle=a^{{\dagger}}(T\xi),

\displaystyle\alpha_{T}(a(\xi))

\displaystyle=a(T^{-1\!\ *}\xi).

(12)

Similarly a bounded operator
G\in B(\mathfrak{h})
yields a densely defined derivation
\delta_{G}
by:

\displaystyle\delta_{G}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi))

\displaystyle=a^{{\dagger}}(G\xi),

\displaystyle\delta_{G}(a(\xi))

\displaystyle=-a(G^{*}\xi).

(13)

In the Fock representation (
3
), Bogoliubov transformations and their derivations can be implemented as (unbounded) operators on the dense subspace
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{h})=\!\ _{\alg}\!\oplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\wedge^{n}\mathfrak{h}
of vectors with finite particle number by multiplicative,
F_{0}
, and additive,
dF_{0}
, second quantization:

\displaystyle\alpha_{T}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\Ad_{F_{0}(G)}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi)),

\displaystyle\delta_{G}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\ad_{dF_{0}(G)}(a^{{\dagger}}(\xi)),

(14)

where

\displaystyle F_{0}(T)\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n}

\displaystyle=T\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge T\eta_{n},

\displaystyle F_{0}(T)\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{0},

(15)

\displaystyle dF_{0}(G)\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{n}\eta_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge G\eta_{m}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta_{n},

\displaystyle dF_{0}(G)\Omega_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

F_{0}
and
dF_{0}
are related by
F_{0}=\exp dF_{0}
via the identification
T=\exp(G)
, and we have the obvious bound:

\displaystyle\|dF_{0}(G)P_{\leq n}\|

\displaystyle\leq n\|G\|.

(16)

Moreover, we can expand
F_{0}
and
dF_{0}
in terms of annihilation and creation operators because:

\displaystyle dF_{0}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}a^{{\dagger}}(G\xi_{i})a(\xi_{i})=a^{{\dagger}}Aa,

(17)

for some orthonormal basis
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of
\mathfrak{h}
, which satisfies:

\displaystyle[dF_{0}(G_{1}),dF_{0}(G_{2})]

\displaystyle=dF_{0}([G_{1},G_{2}]).

(18)

We note that the parity operator is given by
F_{0}(-1)=(-1)^{F}
.

In the self-dual setting,
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h},C)
, the same ideas apply with further constraints on the admissible one-particle operators. Specifically, with respect to the conjugation
C
, we require:

\displaystyle CT^{-1\!\ *}C

\displaystyle=T,

\displaystyle CG^{*}C

\displaystyle=-G.

(19)

If we write
G=iH
instead, we will also have
CH^{*}C=-H
. We denote the analogues of
F_{0}
and
dF_{0}
by
Q_{0}
respectively
dQ_{0}
in this case:

\displaystyle dQ_{0}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\tfrac{1}{2}\Psi(G\xi_{i})^{*}\Psi(\xi_{i})=\tfrac{1}{2}\Psi^{*}A\Psi.

(20)

Starting from the basic case, it is possible to extend the notion of Bogoliubov transformations and their derivations as well as multiplicative and additive second quantization to larger classes of operators on
\mathfrak{h}
, based on the bound:

\displaystyle\|dF_{0}(G)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta_{1})\dots a^{{\dagger}}(\eta_{n})\Omega_{0}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\|\eta_{1}\|\dots\|G\eta_{m}\|\dots\|\eta_{n}\|.

(21)

Important examples, that we will make use of, comprise contractions
\|T\|\leq 1
as well as unbounded (essentially) self-adjoint operators
(H,\mathcal{D}(H))
, such that
G=iH
, or even closed unbounded operators in the case of the generators of the Virasoro algebra.

As pointed out above, we are particularly interested in the implementability of Bogoliubov transformations in the context of quasi-free representations
\pi_{S}
. In this context, a key formula is the additive
normal-ordered second quantization
relative to a pure-state representation
\pi_{S}
,
S^{2}=S
, in analogy with (
17
):

\displaystyle dF_{S}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}:\pi_{S}(a^{{\dagger}}(G\xi_{i})a(\xi_{i})):

(22)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\Big(a^{{\dagger}}(S_{+}G\xi_{i})a(S_{+}\xi_{i}))-a^{{\dagger}}(JS_{-}\xi_{i})a(JS_{-}G\xi_{i}))

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+a^{{\dagger}}(S_{+}G\xi_{i})a^{{\dagger}}(JS_{-}\xi_{i}))+a(JS_{-}G\xi_{i}))a(S_{+}\xi_{i}))\Big)

\displaystyle=a^{{\dagger}}G_{++}a-a^{{\dagger}}G^{t}_{--}a+a^{{\dagger}}G_{+-}a^{{\dagger}}+aG_{-+}a,

with
G^{t}=JG^{*}J
, and similarly,

\displaystyle dQ_{S}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}:\pi_{S}(\tfrac{1}{2}\Psi(G\xi_{i})^{*}\Psi(\xi_{i})):

(23)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a^{{\dagger}}G_{++}a-a^{{\dagger}}G^{T}_{--}a+a^{{\dagger}}G_{+-}a^{{\dagger}}+aG_{-+}a\big)

\displaystyle=a^{{\dagger}}G_{++}a+\tfrac{1}{2}\big(a^{{\dagger}}G_{+-}a^{{\dagger}}+aG_{-+}a\big),

with
G^{T}=CG^{*}C
. Here,
S_{-}=S
,
S_{+}=1-S
, and we use block-matrix notation,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=S_{+}GS_{+}+S_{+}GS_{-}+S_{-}GS_{+}+S_{-}GS_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}G_{++}&G_{+-}\\
G_{-+}&G_{--}\end{pmatrix},

(24)

as well as the standard symbol
:\!\ \cdot\!\ :
for normal ordering with respect to the Fock vacuum
\Omega_{0}
3
3

3

i.e. creation operators to left and annihilation operators to the right with the appropriate signs due to the CAR.
. These formulas show that
dF_{S}(G)
and
dQ_{S}(G)
can be defined as operators on Fock space if the off-diagonal parts
G_{+-}
and
G_{-+}
are in the Hilbert-Schmidt class because:

\displaystyle\|a^{{\dagger}}G_{+-}a^{{\dagger}}P_{\leq n}\|

\displaystyle\leq(n+2)\|G_{+-}\|_{2},

\displaystyle\|aG_{+-}aP_{\leq n}\|

\displaystyle\leq n\|G_{+-}\|_{2}.

(25)

Notably, the formulas (
22
) & (
23
) can be written as:

\displaystyle dF_{S}(G)

\displaystyle=\pi_{S}(dF_{0}(G))-\Tr_{\mathfrak{h}}(G_{--})=\pi_{S}(dF_{0}(G))-\omega_{S}(dF_{0}(G)),

(26)

\displaystyle dQ_{S}(G)

\displaystyle=\pi_{S}(dQ_{0}(G))-\tfrac{1}{2}\Tr_{\mathfrak{h}}(G_{--})=\pi_{S}(dQ_{0}(G))-\omega_{S}(dQ_{0}(G)),

when
G_{--}
is in the trace class. It is a consequence of these formulas that the commutator identity (
18
) acquires an additional central term, called the
Schwinger cocycle

[
66
]
:

\displaystyle c_{S}(G_{1},G_{2})

\displaystyle=\Tr_{\mathfrak{h}}([G_{1},G_{2}]_{--})=\Tr_{\mathfrak{h}}((G_{1})_{-+}(G_{2})_{+-}-(G_{2})_{-+}(G_{1})_{+-}),

(27)

such that

\displaystyle[dF_{S}(G_{1}),dF_{S}(G_{2})]

\displaystyle=dF_{S}([G_{1},G_{2}])+c_{S}(G_{1},G_{2}),

(28)

\displaystyle [dQ_{S}(G_{1}),dQ_{S}(G_{2})]

\displaystyle=dQ_{S}([G_{1},G_{2}])+\tfrac{1}{2}c_{S}(G_{1},G_{2}).

It precisely the Schwinger cocycle
c_{S}
that leads to the central charge in the commutation relations of the Virasoro generators. For operators
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
with off-diagonal parts in the Hilbert-Schmidt class the Schwinger cocycle satisfies the obvious bound:

\displaystyle|c_{S}(G_{1},G_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\big(\|(G_{1})_{-+}\|_{2}\|(G_{2})_{+-}\|_{2}+\|(G_{2})_{-+}\|_{2}\|(G_{1})_{+-}\|_{2}\big).

(29)

2.2
Lattice fermions at different scales

We describe spatial fermions
4
4

4

Or time-zero fermions as opposed to spacetime fermions.
with
s
components at a (dyadic) resolution
\varepsilon_{N}=2^{-N}\varepsilon_{0}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, for some basic length scale
\varepsilon_{0}
in a
d
-dimensional hypercubic volume,
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}=(S^{1}_{L})^{\times d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/2L\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, of size
\vol\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}=(2L)^{d}
,
L>0
, by the one-particle space,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s},

\displaystyle\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\langle\xi_{x},\eta_{x}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{s}},

(30)

associated with the lattice,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{N}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}\{-L_{N},\dots,L_{N}-1\}^{d}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{N}L_{N}

\displaystyle=L.

(31)

The subscript
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}
refers to the unitary action of the translations
\tau^{(N)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}:\Lambda_{N}\act\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
given by:

\displaystyle\tau^{(N)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}|x}(\xi)_{y}

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}1)^{\big[\frac{y-x}{2L}\big]}\xi_{y-x},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

(32)

where we allow for the occurrence of a non-trivial phase which we also refer to as
boundary condition
5
5

5

We restrict attention boundary conditions associated with phases
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}1
, i.e. unitary representations of the double cover of
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
, because we are interested in translation-invariant quadratic Hamiltonians in the following, but more general phases given by other coverings of
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
are conceivable.
, because it encodes whether
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
can be considered as periodic (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}
) or anti-periodic (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}
) on
\Lambda_{N}
. These two types of boundary conditions are also referred to as
Ramond sector
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}
) and
Neveu-Schwarz sector
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}
)
[
21
,
26
]
.

We denote the corresponding algebras of lattice fermions, complex and Majorana, by:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},C),

(33)

where
C:\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is the charge conjugation that we only specify explicitly in the next subsection. We use the notation,

\displaystyle a^{(j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=a(\delta^{(N)}_{x,j}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{x}

\displaystyle=\Psi(\delta^{(N)}_{x,j}),

(34)

for the special set of generators of
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
associated with the standard basis of
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\delta^{(N)}_{x,j}

\displaystyle=\delta^{(N)}_{x}\otimes(0,\dots,\!\!\!\!\!\!\underbrace{1}_{j\textup{th component}}\!\!\!\!\!\!,\dots,0)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{N},j=1,...,s.

(35)

The lattice Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}^{(j)}(k)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{-ikx}\xi^{(j)}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle e_{k,j},\xi\rangle_{N},

\displaystyle\xi=(\xi^{(1)},\dots,\xi^{(s)})

\displaystyle\in\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s},

(36)

provides a unitary identification
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\cong\ell^{2}(\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},(2L_{N})^{-d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s}
where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\pi}{L}\{-L_{N},...,0,...,L_{N}-1\}^{d},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\pi}{L}\{-L_{N}+\tfrac{1}{2},...,0,...,L_{N}-\tfrac{1}{2}\}^{d},

(37)

is the dual momentum-space lattice
\Lambda_{N}
, and

\displaystyle e_{k,j}

\displaystyle=e^{ik(\!\ .\!\ )}\otimes(0,\dots,\!\!\!\!\!\!\underbrace{1}_{j\textup{th component}}\!\!\!\!\!\!,\dots,0),

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},j=1,\dots,s,

(38)

is the plane-wave basis of
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

We extend the Fourier transform and its inverse to
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
by linearity:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(j)}_{k}

\displaystyle=a(e_{k,j})=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{-ikx}\phi^{(j)}_{x},

\displaystyle a^{(j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=\hat{a}(e_{-x,j})=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N}}e^{ikx}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{k},

(39)

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}^{(j)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Psi(e_{k,j})=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{-ikx}\Psi^{(j)}_{x},

\displaystyle\Psi^{(j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=\hat{\Psi}(e_{-x,j})=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N}}e^{ikx}\hat{\Psi}^{(j)}_{k},

for
x\in\Lambda_{N}
,
k\in\Gamma_{N}
,
j=1,\dots,s
. Here, we also introduced the dual plane-wave basis
\{e_{x,j}\ |\ x\in\Lambda_{N},j=1,\dots,s\}
by analogy with (
38
). Thus, the lattice Fourier transform provides us with
*
-isomorphims,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{N.\pm}

\displaystyle\!\cong\!\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\ell^{2}(\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},(2L_{N})^{-d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle\!\cong\!\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\ell^{2}(\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},(2L_{N})^{-d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s},C),

(40)

which can be expressed by:
\hat{a}(\hat{\xi})=a(\xi)
respectively
\hat{\Psi}(\hat{\xi})=\Psi(\xi)
for
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

In view of the construction of the scaling limit in Section
3
, we also allow for
N=\infty
, where we put:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s}\cong\ell^{2}(\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},(2L)^{-d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{s},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\pi}{L}\mathbb{Z}^{d},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\pi}{L}(\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2})^{d},

(41)

with the continuum Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}^{(j)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{L}[\xi^{(j)}]_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}}dx\!\ e^{-ikx}\xi^{(j)}_{x}=\langle e_{k,j},f\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})},

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

(42)

for periodic and anti-periodic functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. As above, we have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},C).

(43)

2.3
The free lattice Dirac Hamiltonian in 1+1-dimensions

Since our principal interest lies with models that exhibit local conformal symmetries in the scaling limit, we restrict from this point on to one spatial dimension (
d=1
) and two-component fermions (
s=1
). Specifically, we undertake a detailed analysis of a discretized version of the free Dirac Hamiltonian on the lattice
\Lambda_{N}
as various models of conformal field theory can be realized in terms of free fermions in the continuum
[
21
,
26
,
95
,
91
]
. In the staggered lattice approximation
6
6

6

We use the forward and backward difference to approximate the derivative of the two components respectively. This avoids the notorious fermion doubling in lattice approximations of continuum fermion models.

[
87
]
the massive Dirac-Hamiltonian as an element of
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is given by:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\left(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(2)}_{x}-a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}+\textup{h.c.}+\lambda_{N}\left(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(1)}_{x}-a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}\right)\right).

(44)

Here,
\lambda_{N}\geq 0
is a dimensionless “lattice mass” parameter, and “h.c.” denotes the hermitean conjugate of the preceding terms. Invoking the lattice Fourier transform, the Hamiltonian (
44
) can be written in
2\times 2
-matrix notation as a second quantized operator:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!*}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{N}&e^{-ik\varepsilon_{N}}-1\\
e^{ik\varepsilon_{N}}-1&-\lambda_{N}\end{pmatrix}}_{=n_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\cdot\sigma=h^{(N)}_{0}(k)}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k}\end{pmatrix}=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}dF_{0}(h^{(N)}_{0}),

(45)

where
n_{\lambda_{N}}(k)=(\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)e_{x}+\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)e_{y}+\lambda_{N}e_{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
\sigma
is the vector of Pauli matrices:

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\sigma_{y}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\sigma_{z}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(46)

The spectral decomposition of the
one-particle Hamiltonian

h^{(N)}_{0}(k)
,

\displaystyle h^{(N)}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\Big(P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)-P^{(-)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\Big),

(47)

is given in terms of the spectral projections
7
7

7

Apart from
k=0
and
\lambda_{N}=0
where
h^{(N)}_{0}(0)=0
which is consistent with
P^{(\pm)}_{0}(0)=\tfrac{1}{2}\1_{2}
.
,

\displaystyle P^{(\pm)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)}\big(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\1_{2}\pm h^{(N)}_{0}(k)\big)

(48)

with the lattice dispersion relation:

\displaystyle\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{N}^{2}+4\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(49)

At
k=0
, we have
h^{(N)}_{0}(0)=\lambda_{N}\sigma_{z}
, and we diagonalize
h^{(N)}_{0}(k)
for all
k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
by:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)}\Big)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)+\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}&i\sign(k)(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)-\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{i}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k}\\
i\frac{2\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}{(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)+\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{\frac{i}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k}&\sign(k)\frac{2\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}{(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)-\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\end{pmatrix}

(50)

\displaystyle=\cosh(\vartheta_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\cosh(\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k})&i\sinh(\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k})e^{-\frac{i}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k}\\
i\sinh(\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k})e^{\frac{i}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k}&\cosh(\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k})\end{pmatrix},

i.e.
\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)^{*}h^{(N)}_{0}(k)\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)=\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\sigma_{z}
. In the second line, we introduced lattice rapidities,
2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)=\lambda_{N}\sinh(\vartheta_{k})
, to exemplify the unitarity of
\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)
for
\lambda_{N}\neq 0
. Thus, the Bogoliubov transformation,

\displaystyle(\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k},\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k})^{t}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)^{*}(\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k},\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k})^{t},

(51)

diagonalizes the Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}_{0}
:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)(\hat{c}^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k}\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}-\hat{c}^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k}\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}).

(52)

The many-body ground state of
H^{(N)}_{0}
, i.e. the lattice vacuum
\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
at scale
N
, corresponds to the half-filled Fock vacuum (
Fermi sea
):

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k}\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\forall k

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}.

(53)

The unitary that implements the Bogoliubov transform (
51
) and relates the lattice vacuum
\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
to the Fock vacuum of
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is given by:

\displaystyle U_{\lambda_{N}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\prod_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\!\exp\!\Big(\!\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\big(\nu_{k}\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k}\hat{a}^{(1)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{k}\!\!+\!\mu_{k}\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k}\hat{a}^{(2)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{k}\big)\!\!\Big)\!=\!\exp\!\Big(\!\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\!\nu_{k}\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k}\hat{a}^{(1)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{k}\!\!+\!\mu_{k}\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k}\hat{a}^{(2)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{k}\Big)

(54)

where

\displaystyle(\nu_{k}\mu_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\cosh^{-1}\Big(\tfrac{\cosh(\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{k})}{\cosh(\vartheta_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big),\ \big(\tfrac{\nu_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=-ie^{-\frac{i}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k}.

(55)

It is evident from the expression for
U_{\lambda_{N}}
that the lattice vacuum is of even parity:
(-1)^{F}\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\!=\!\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. Alternatively, we can invoke another Bogoliubov transformation, commonly used in quantum field theory,

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{c}^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k},

\displaystyle\forall k

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

(56)

to obtain a description of the Fermi sea as a standard Fock vacuum:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{(1)}_{k}\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{(2)}_{k}\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\forall k

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}.

(57)

The Bogoliubov transformation (
56
) exemplifies the need for an additive energy renormalization of the Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}_{0}
in the scaling limit
N\rightarrow\infty
:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)(\tilde{c}^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k}\tilde{c}^{(1)}_{k}+\tilde{c}^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{k}\tilde{c}^{(2)}_{k})-\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k).

(58)

The lattice vacuum
\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
determines a quasi-free state on the fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta)\!)

\displaystyle\!=\!(\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\!,\hat{a}(\hat{\xi})\hat{a}^{{\dagger}}(\hat{\eta})\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\langle\hat{\xi}_{k},\!P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}}\!(k)\hat{\eta}_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\!=\!\langle\xi,\!(1\!-\!S^{(N)}_{0})\eta\rangle_{N},

(59)

where the one-particle operator is specified in momentum-space by
S^{(N)}_{0}(k)=P^{(-)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)
.

From this expression it is easy to obtain the massless lattice vacuum in the limit
\lambda_{N}\rightarrow 0+
, although the Ramond sector acquires a degeneracy in the zero mode (
k=0
) because
h^{(N)}_{0}(0)=0
:

\displaystyle P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}_{2}+\sign(k)(-\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)\sigma_{x}+\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)\sigma_{y}))

(60)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&-i\sign(k)e^{-\frac{i}{2}k\varepsilon_{N}}\\
i\sign(k)e^{\frac{i}{2}k\varepsilon_{N}}&1\end{pmatrix},

with the convention
\sign(0)=0
. This way,
P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(0)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathds{1}_{2}
reflects a uniform probability distribution of the quasi-free state
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
at
k=0
compatible with the chiral decomposition of
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
(see Section
2.4
). But, the state is not pure because
P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}
is not a projection
8
8

8

Another possibility is to enforce purity by putting
\sign(0)=1
to have
P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(0)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}_{2}+\sigma_{y})
which is also compatible with the chiral decomposition.
.

2.4
Chiral decomposition

The chiral parts of the lattice fermion algebras result from applying the chiral projectors
p_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}_{2}\pm\sigma_{y})
9
9

9

In our parametrization of the Dirac Hamiltonian
\gamma_{5}=\sigma_{y}
.
to the one-particle space
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}=\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes p_{+}\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes p_{-}\mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathfrak{h}^{(+)}_{N,\pm}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{(-)}_{N,\pm}.

(61)

Using the eigenvectors
e_{\pm}=2^{-\frac{1}{2}}(e_{1}\pm ie_{2})
of
p_{\pm}
, we can introduce the complex chiral fermions,
\mathfrak{A}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
, by

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}(\xi)=a(\xi e_{\pm})

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{1}{2}}(a^{(1)}(\xi)\mp ia^{(2)}(\xi))=\langle e_{\pm},(a^{(1)}(\xi),a^{(2)}(\xi))^{t}\rangle.

(62)

At this point, we also fix the charge conjugation
C
(for all
N\leq\infty
) as:

\displaystyle C\xi

\displaystyle=\sigma_{z}\overline{\xi},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

(63)

which acts as complex conjugation with respect to the chiral decomposition:

\displaystyle C\xi

\displaystyle=\overline{\xi_{+}}e_{+}+\overline{\xi_{-}}e_{-},

(64)

for
\xi_{\pm}=\langle e_{\pm},\xi\rangle
and, thus,
a(C\xi)=\psi_{+}(\overline{\xi_{+}})+\psi_{-}(\overline{\xi_{-}})
. This should be contrasted with the standard conjugation,
\xi\mapsto\overline{\xi}
, on
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
that satisfies
a(\overline{\xi})=\psi_{+}(\overline{\xi_{-}})+\psi_{-}(\overline{\xi_{+}})
.

In contrast with infinite-volume continuum theory, the massless ground-state projector
P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}
does not factorize w.r.t. the chiral parts, in other words the massless lattice vacuum is entangled relative to the chiral parts:

\displaystyle p_{\pm}P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)p_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(1\pm\sign(k)\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k))p_{\pm},

(65)

\displaystyle p_{\pm}P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)p_{\mp}

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{i}{2}\sign(k)\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_{z}\pm i\sigma_{x})}_{=n_{\pm}}.

In terms of these, the Hamiltonian (
45
) takes the form:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)&\!\!\!\!-i(\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N}\\
i(\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N}&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)\end{pmatrix}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix},

(66)

which, in the massless case (
\lambda_{N}=0
), is compatible (
19
) with the charge conjugation
C
which allows for the identification of the self-dual components, i.e. the Majorana fermions
\mathfrak{B}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},C)
, of the complex massless chiral fields (see Section
2.6
).

Because of the entanglement of the massless lattice vacuum
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
relative to the chiral decomposition, its restriction to chiral components defines non-pure quasi-free states (
p_{\pm}P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)p_{\pm}
is positive but not a projection):

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi)\psi_{\pm}^{\dagger}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\tfrac{1}{2}(1\pm\sign(k)\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k))\overline{\hat{\xi}(k)}\hat{\zeta}(k),

(67)

for
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\cong\ell^{2}(\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},(2L_{N})^{-1})
.

2.5
The Majorana mass term

As stated above, the Hamiltonian (
44
) is a discretized version of the massive Dirac Hamiltonian using the algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, which is only compatible with the Majorana fermions
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
for vanishing lattice mass
\lambda_{N}=0
. To allow for a massive Hamiltonian in terms of the Majorana fermions
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, the mass term (proportional to
\lambda_{N}
) needs to be compatible with self-duality (
19
). This is achieved by replacing the Dirac mass term in (
44
) with:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{\textup{mass}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\lambda_{N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\!\!\!\left(a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(1)}_{x}+a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}\!\right)=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\lambda_{N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\!(-i)\!\left(\psi^{\dagger}_{+|x}\psi_{-|x}-\psi^{\dagger}_{-|x}\psi_{+|x}\right)

(68)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}0&\!\!\!\!-i\lambda_{N}\\
i\lambda_{N}&0\end{pmatrix}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix},

which results in the modified spectral projections,

\displaystyle P^{(\pm)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)=\tfrac{1}{2\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)}(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\mathds{1}_{2}\pm n_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\cdot\sigma),

(69)

with
n_{\lambda_{N}}(k)=(\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)e_{x}+\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)e_{y}+\lambda_{N}e_{x}
, and the modified dispersion relation,

\displaystyle\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{N}^{2}+4(1-\lambda_{N})\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(70)

The modified spectral projections satisfy
CP^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}}=P^{(-)}_{\lambda_{N}}C
, consistent with (
8
), and, thus, (
59
) defines a quasi-free state, also denoted
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
by a slight abuse of notation, on
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

2.6
The self-dual components of the lattice Majorana Hamiltonian

The Hamiltonian with the Majorana mass term,

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\left(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(2)}_{x}-a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}+\textup{h.c.}+\lambda_{N}\left(a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(1)}_{x}+a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}\right)\right)

(71)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\!\!\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)&\!\!\!\!-i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})\\
i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)\end{pmatrix}}_{=h^{(N)}_{\sd}(k)}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\psi}_{+|k}\\
\hat{\psi}_{-|k}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}dF_{0}(h^{(N)}_{\sd}),

is compatible with the charge conjugation (
63
). Moreover, the projections on positive and negative momenta (with an appropriate modification in the Ramond sector),

\displaystyle(P^{\pm}\hat{\xi})_{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{matrix}\theta(\pm k)\hat{\xi}_{k}&k\neq 0,-\tfrac{\pi}{\varepsilon_{N}}\\
\Re(e^{\mp\frac{i}{4}\pi}\hat{\xi}_{k})e^{\pm\frac{i}{4}\pi}&k=0,-\tfrac{\pi}{\varepsilon_{N}}\end{matrix}\right.,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

(72)

are basis projections for
C
(see (
5
) and below):

\displaystyle(CP^{\pm}\hat{\xi})(k)

\displaystyle=((1-P^{\pm})C\hat{\xi})(k).

(73)

Therefore, it is possible to formulate
H^{(N)}_{0}
in terms of four Majorana fermions,
\Psi_{\pm},:\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{\pm}:\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\pm}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\pm}(P^{+}\xi)+\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\pm}(CP^{-}\xi),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{\pm}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\pm}(P^{-}\xi)+\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\pm}(CP^{+}\xi),

(74)

for
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, which correspond to the restrictions of the complex chiral fermions
\psi_{\pm}
to positive and negative momenta. Then
H^{(N)}_{0}
is given by self-dual second quantization:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\big(dQ_{0}(h^{(N)}_{\sd})+d\tilde{Q}_{0}(h^{(N)}_{\sd})\big),

\displaystyle[dQ_{0}(h^{(N)}_{\sd}),d\tilde{Q}_{0}(h^{(N)}_{\sd})]

\displaystyle=0,

(75)

In view of the diagonalization (
52
) of
H^{(N)}_{0}
, it is worth noting that the Majorana fermions (
74
) can be interpreted as the self-dual components of
c^{(1)},c^{(2)}
in the (formal) scaling limit
N\rightarrow\infty
of the massless case
\lambda_{N}=0
:

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma|k}

\displaystyle\!=\!\left\{\begin{matrix}\theta(+k)\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}+\theta(-k)\hat{c}^{(1)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}&\sigma\!=\!+\\[2.84544pt]
\theta(+k)\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}+\theta(-k)\hat{c}^{(2)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}&\sigma\!=\!-\end{matrix}\right.,

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\Psi}}_{\sigma|k}

\displaystyle\!=\!\left\{\begin{matrix}\theta(-k)\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}+\theta(+k)\hat{c}^{(2)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}&\sigma\!=\!+\\[2.84544pt]
\theta(-k)\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}+\theta(+k)\hat{c}^{(1)\!\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}&\sigma\!=\!-\end{matrix}\right.,

(76)

for
k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. Furthermore, it is evident from (
75
) that
H^{(N)}_{0}
should decouple into four massless Majorana fermions in this limit.

2.7
Equivalence with the transverse XY model

In view of our companion article
[
72
]
that discusses our results in relation to the quantum simulation of CFTs, we provide some details on how to map the fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
together with the Dirac Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}_{0}
to a
Pauli algebra
,
\mathfrak{P}_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\pm}}=\otimes_{x\in\Lambda_{N+1}}M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, in which each local matrix factor resembles a (logical) qubit, using a Jordan-Wigner isomorphism. Under this mapping the Dirac Hamiltonian becomes that of an XY model with a transverse field.

To begin with, we identify the algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
of two-component fermion with a one-component fermion algebra,
\mathfrak{A}_{\SDC}(\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N+1})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
, on the doubled lattice
\Lambda_{N+1}
:

\displaystyle b_{x}

\displaystyle=a^{(1)}_{x},

\displaystyle b_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}

\displaystyle=a^{(2){\dagger}}_{x},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{N},

(77)

which exploits the symmetry of
H^{(N)}_{0}
given by:
(a^{(1)}_{x},a^{(2)}_{x})\mapsto(a^{(2){\dagger}}_{x},a^{(1){\dagger}}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}})
for all
x\in\Lambda_{N}
. In terms of
b,b^{{\dagger}}
, the Hamiltonian (
44
) is mapped to:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N+1}}\hskip-7.11317pt\big(b_{x}b_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}-b^{{\dagger}}_{x}b^{{\dagger}}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}+\lambda_{N}(b^{{\dagger}}_{x}b_{x}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathds{1}_{2})\big),

(78)

with periodic or anti-periodic boundary conditions in accordance with
(a^{(1)},a^{(2)})
. The translation invariance of
H^{(N)}_{0}
on
\Lambda_{N+1}
subsumes the translation invariance on
\Lambda_{N}
and special field-exchange symmetry from above. In the momentum space representation, the relation (
77
) between the single-component fermion
a
and two-component fermion
(a^{(1)},a^{(2)})
is given by:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\hat{b}_{k}+\hat{b}_{k+\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon_{N+1}}}),

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}e^{i\varepsilon_{N+1}k}(\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}_{-k}-\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}_{-k+\frac{\pi}{\varepsilon_{N+1}}}),

(79)

where the two-component fermion can be considered to be periodically extended from
\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
to
\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

A follow-up
Jordan-Wigner isomorphism
,

\displaystyle b_{x}

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\Lambda_{N+1}\\
-L\leq y<x\end{subarray}}\sigma^{(1)}_{y}\Big)\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma^{(3)}_{x}+i\sigma^{(2)}_{x}),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{N+1},
