(80)

yields a soluble transverse XY model (cp.
[
65
]
):

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N+1}}\hskip-7.11317pt\big(\sigma^{(3)}_{x}\sigma^{(3)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}-\sigma^{(2)}_{x}\sigma^{(2)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}+\lambda_{N}\sigma^{(1)}_{x}\big)

(81)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt-\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\pm}1{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\pm}(-1)^{F})(\sigma^{(3)}_{-L}\sigma^{(3)}_{L-\varepsilon_{N+1}}-\sigma^{(2)}_{-L}\sigma^{(2)}_{L-\varepsilon_{N+1}}),

where (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\pm}
) indicates the fermion boundary conditions while (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\pm}
) labels the complementing spin boundary conditions, such that boundary term in the second line vanishes on the lattice vacuum
\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

We can rephrase the diagonalization of
H^{(N)}_{0}
as given in (
52
) in terms of the Fourier transform of the one-component fermion
b,b^{{\dagger}}
:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4L}\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N+1,-,>0}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{b}_{k}\\
\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{N}&-i2\sin(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)\\
i2\sin(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)&-\lambda_{N}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{b}_{k}\\
\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}.

(82)

The diagonalizing Bogoliubov transformation is completely analogous to (
50
) for
k>0
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)

\displaystyle=\Big(\tfrac{1}{2\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)+\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}&i\sign(k)(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)-\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
i\frac{2\sin(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)}{(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)+\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}&\sign(k)\frac{2\sin(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)}{(\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)-\lambda_{N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\end{pmatrix},

(83)

which defines the diagonalizing fermion,
(\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}^{{\dagger}}_{-k})^{t}=\gamma_{\lambda_{N}}(k)^{\dagger}(\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}^{{\dagger}}_{-k})^{t}
,
leading to:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4L}\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\hskip-7.11317pt\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\hat{c}^{{\dagger}}_{k}\hat{c}_{k}-\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\hskip-7.11317pt\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k).

(84)

If we normalize the fermion,
\tilde{c}_{k}=(2L_{N+1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}_{k}
, and subject it to another inverse Jordan-Wigner transform similar to (
80
), we can write

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\omega_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\tilde{\sigma}^{(1)}_{k}.

(85)

Therefore, it is possible to express the lattice vacuum as a qubit product state:
\Omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\otimes_{k\in\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}|\!\leftarrow\rangle
, which allows for the initialization of a quantum simulation of (
81
) in its many-body ground state following
[
90
]
.

3
Scaling limits of lattice fermions

We now explain how to implement the scaling limit,
N\rightarrow\infty
, rigorously by operator-algebraic renormalization
[
85
,
10
]
which in turn allows us to give precise statements about the convergence of lattice quantities, e.g. correlation functions with respect to the lattice vacuum
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
or finite-scale Bogoliubov transformations and their implementers.

Following the recipe in
[
69
]
, we define the scaling limit of lattice fermions, complex and Majorana, by a renormalization group procedure in terms of an inductive system of unital, injective
*
-morphisms,

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{N+1}:\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N+1,\pm},

\displaystyle\alpha^{N+1}_{N+2}\circ\alpha^{N}_{N+1}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{N}_{N+2},

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(86)

and similarly for
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. In the following we state the formulas for
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
only, but they equally apply to
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

In present setting, we define the
renormalization group

\{\alpha^{N}_{N+1}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as Bogoliubov transformations associated with an inductive system of isometries between one-particle spaces,

\displaystyle R^{N}_{N+1}:\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{N+1,\pm},

\displaystyle R^{N+1}_{N+2}\circ R^{N}_{N+1}

\displaystyle=R^{N}_{N+2}

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(87)

with the additional requirement,
R^{N}_{N+1}C=CR^{N}_{N+1}
, in the self-dual case.

Because of the general properties of CAR algebras
[
9
,
26
]
, we obtain the inductive-limit objects:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}.

(88)

At the level of states,
\omega^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A}^{*}_{N,\pm}
, the
renormalization group flow
is defined by:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{M}

\displaystyle=\omega^{(N+M)}\circ\alpha^{N}_{N+M},

\displaystyle N,M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(89)

A scaling limit of a family of lattice states
\{\omega^{(N)}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\omega^{(N)}_{M},

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(90)

which is automatically projectively consistent,
\omega^{N+1}_{\infty}\circ\alpha^{N}_{N+1}=\omega^{N}_{\infty}
, by continuity
[
69
,
10
]
if it exists as a weak
∗
-limit and, thus, defines a state
\omega^{(\infty)}_{\infty}
on
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. In this sense, the inductive-limit objects (
88
) serve as carrier spaces of the scaling limit of a family
\{\omega^{(N)}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

The observation that the inductive-limit objects (
88
) can be characterized as the sets of
\alpha
- respectively
R
-convergent sequences
[
22
]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|O_{N}-\alpha^{M}_{N}(O_{M})\|

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\|\xi_{N}-R^{M}_{N}(\xi_{M})\|_{N}

\displaystyle=0,

(91)

for
O_{N}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
respectively
\xi_{N}\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, motivates the following definition of the scaling limit of sequences of composite operators with limits possibly not in
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
10
10

10

In
[
22
]
the concept of convergent sequences is discussed for generalized inductive systems of Banach spaces in the context of mean-field limits.
.

Definition 3.1

(Convergent sequences of operators)
.

Let
\{p_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of semi-norms,
p_{N}:\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. A sequence of operators
\{O_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
with
O_{N}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is called
\alpha
-convergent with respect to the family
\{p_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
if:

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}p_{N}(O_{N}-\alpha^{M}_{N}(O_{M}))

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly, let
\{q_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of semi-norms,
q_{N}:B(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we call a sequence of operators
\{o_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, with
o_{N}\in B(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
,
R
-convergent with respect to the family
\{q_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
if:

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}q_{N}(o_{N}-R^{M}_{N}o_{M}R^{M}_{N}{}^{*})

\displaystyle=0.

Of course,
R
-convergence for one-particle operators is related to
\alpha
-convergence by the observation:

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{M}(dF_{0}(o_{N}))

\displaystyle=dF_{0}(R^{N}_{M}o_{N}R^{N}_{M}{}^{*}).

(92)

In Section
4
, we invoke families of semi-norms that are induced by semi-norms
p_{\infty}
and
q_{\infty}
on
B(\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}))
respectively
B(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
:

\displaystyle p_{N}

\displaystyle=p_{\infty}\circ\alpha^{N}_{\infty},

\displaystyle q_{N}

\displaystyle=q_{\infty}\circ(R^{N}_{\infty}(\!\ \cdot\!\ )R^{N}_{\infty}{}^{*}).

(93)

Specific examples are strong operator semi-norms (for
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
),

\displaystyle p_{\infty}(O)

\displaystyle=\|Oa^{{\dagger}}(R^{M}_{\infty}(\xi_{1}))...a^{{\dagger}}(R^{M}_{\infty}(\xi_{n}))\Omega_{0}\|,

\displaystyle\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}_{M,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},j=1,...,n,

(94)

\displaystyle q_{\infty}(o)

\displaystyle=\|oR^{M}_{\infty}(\xi)\|_{\infty},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}_{M,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

for operators
O
on
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
or operators
o
on the one-particle space
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. This way, we can study the convergence of sequences of operators at finite scales such as the lattice Dirac Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}_{0}
and its one-particle version
h^{(N)}_{0}
, which should have limits that are unbounded operators in the scaling limit.

3.1
Wavelet renormalization group

In the following, we make use of a specific realization of the renormalization group using the theory of wavelets by adapting the constructions in
[
69
]
to the fermionic setting.

To this end, we consider a compactly supported orthonormal
scaling function
,
s\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\|s\|_{L^{1}}=1
, associated with a wavelet basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that satisfies the
scaling equation

[
20
,
67
]
:

\displaystyle s(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{n}2^{\frac{1}{2}}s(2x-n),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R},

(95)

where the coefficients
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are called a
low-pass filter
11
11

11

The compact support of
s
enforces a that the low-pass filter has only finitely many non-vanishing elements.
. Since our finite-scale fermion algebras,
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, describe quantum systems in the finite volume
S^{1}_{L}
, we denote by,

\displaystyle s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s(\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}(x+2Lm)),

(96)

the rescaled and
2L
-periodized scaling function, which yield a wavelet basis of
L^{2}(S^{1}_{L})
and satisfy in analogy with (
95
):

\displaystyle s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}\Lambda_{N+1}}h_{n}s^{(\varepsilon_{N+1})}(x-\varepsilon_{N+1}n),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in S^{1}_{L}.

(97)

The wavelet renormalization group is now defined as follows:

Definition 3.2

(Wavelet renormalization group)
.

Given a compactly supported orthonormal scaling function
s\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with low-pass filter
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, let,

\displaystyle R^{N}_{N+1}(\xi^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\xi^{(j)}_{x}\sum_{n\in\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}\Lambda_{N+1}}h_{n}\delta^{(N+1)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}n},

(98)

and

\displaystyle R^{N}_{\infty}(\xi^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\xi^{(j)}_{x}\underbrace{s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(\cdot\!\ -x)}_{:=s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{x}}=s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}\xi^{(j)},

(99)

for
\xi=(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, where we use the notation
\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}
to denote the convolution with respect to the lattice
\Lambda_{N}
. The
wavelet renormalization group
is densely defnined by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{N+1}(a(\xi))

\displaystyle=a(R^{N}_{N+1}(\xi)),

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(a(\xi))

\displaystyle=a(R^{N}_{\infty}(\xi)),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}.

(100)

We summarize some important properties of the maps
R^{N}_{N+1}
that follow immediately from the properties of a (compactly supported) scaling function and its associated wavelet basis, cf.
[
67
,
20
,
69
]
:

Proposition 3.3
.

The one-particle map of the wavelet renormalization group has the following properties:
R^{N}_{N+1}:\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{N+1,\pm}
is

1.

well-defined,

2.

C
-compatible,

3.

isometric, i.e.
R^{N}_{N+1}{}^{\!\!*}\circ R^{N}_{N+1}=\1_{\mathfrak{h}_{N}}
,

4.

asymptotically compatible, i.e.
R^{N+1}_{\infty}\circ R^{N}_{N+1}=R^{N}_{\infty}
.

Moreover, we have
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\cong L^{2}(S^{1}_{L})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and
R^{N}_{\infty}{}^{*}\circ R^{N}_{\infty}=\1_{\mathfrak{h}_{N}}
.

For our purposes it is convenient to explicitly derive the form of the wavelet renormalization groups in the momentum-space representation via the Fourier transform, cf. (
36
) & (
42
):

\displaystyle R^{N}_{N+1}(\hat{\xi})_{k}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{1}{2}}m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)(\hat{\xi}_{\per})_{k},

\displaystyle R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\xi})_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}k)(\hat{\xi}_{\per})_{k}.

(101)

where
m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)=2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{n}e^{-ik\varepsilon_{N+1}n}
(considered as periodic on
\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
), and
\hat{\xi}_{\per}
denotes the periodic extension of
\hat{\xi}
from
\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
to
\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
respectively
\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. The asymptotic compatibility and various convergence results, as we show in Section
4
, turn out to be a consequence of (applying the Fourier transform to (
95
)):

\displaystyle\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}\!\ .\!\ )

\displaystyle=m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+1}\!\ .\!\ )\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N+1}\!\ .\!\ )=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{n=1}^{M}m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+n}\!\ .\!\ ),

(102)

where the infinite product converges absolutely and uniformly on compact sets
[
20
]
. But, the convergence also holds in arbitrary Sobolev-type subspaces of
L^{2}(S^{1}_{L})
provided the scaling function is regular enough,
s\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
,
\alpha>0
, e.g. those of the Daubechies family, see Lemma
3.7
below.

As we are not only interested in the scaling limit of the fermions,
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, but also that of implementers of Bogoliubov transformations and their generators, such as
dF_{S}(G)
and
dQ_{S}(Q)
given in (
22
) & (
23
), we introduce a modified version of the maps
R^{N}_{N+1}
adapted to the fact that the generators are quadratic in the fermions (i.e.
currents
). To this end, consider the spaces,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{N},\mathbb{C}^{D})

\displaystyle=\{X:\Lambda_{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{D}\}\cong\mathfrak{l}(\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}},\mathbb{C}^{D}),

(103)

of
\mathbb{C}^{D}
-valued differential loops (periodic boundary conditions) together with the obvious extension of the (lattice) Fourier transform.

Definition 3.4

(Wavelet renormalization for differential loops)
.

Given a compactly supported orthonormal scaling function
s\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as in Definition
3.2
. The wavelet renormalization group for
\mathbb{C}^{D}
-valued loops is defined by:

\displaystyle S^{N}_{N+1}(\hat{X}^{(j)})_{k}

\displaystyle=2m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+1}k)(\hat{X}^{(j)}_{\per})_{k},

\displaystyle S^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{X}^{(j)})_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}k)(\hat{X}^{(j)}_{\per})_{k},

(104)

for
X\in\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{N},\mathbb{C}^{D})
.

In view of Proposition
3.3
, we note that the wavelet renormalization group for currents also satisfies asymptotic compatibility:

\displaystyle S^{N+1}_{\infty}\circ S^{N}_{N+1}

\displaystyle=S^{N}_{\infty},

(105)

but we refrain from formulating an associated (topological) inductive limit and simply note that
\im S^{N}_{\infty}\subset\mathfrak{l}^{\alpha}(S^{1}_{L},\mathbb{C}^{D})
is a finite-dimensional subspace of differential
\mathbb{C}^{D}
-valued
C^{\alpha}
-loops associated with the span of
s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}
and its
\Lambda_{N}
-translates.

Clearly, the difference between
R^{N}_{\infty}
and
S^{N}_{\infty}
is the geometrical scaling factor
\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}
respectively
\varepsilon_{N}
which reflects the fact that fermions are half densities while generators are densities.

3.1.1
Quasi-local structure

By analogy with case of lattice scalar fields, the wavelet renormalization group is compatible with the real-space anti-local structure of the fermions on the lattice and in the continuum. The simple reason for this compatibility is the finite length of the low-pass filter
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, which entails that according to (
98
) and (
100
) the fermion and Majorana algebras of a single lattice site at a given scale,
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(x)
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(x)
,
x\in\Lambda_{N}
, generated by
a_{x},a^{{\dagger}}_{x}
respectively
\Psi_{x}
, are mapped into localized algebras at the successive scale,
\mathfrak{A}_{N+1,\pm}(I_{x})
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N+1,\pm}(I_{x})
, generated by
a_{y},a^{{\dagger}}_{y}
respectively
\Psi_{y}
for
y\in I_{x}\cap\Lambda_{N+1}
, where
I_{x}\subset S^{1}_{L}
is an interval determined by the length of the low-pass filter.

This observation allows for the following definition.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
I\subset S^{1}_{L}
be an open interval, and denote by
\Lambda_{N}\cap I
denote the subset of those
x
of intersection as subsets of
S^{1}_{L}
such that
x+\supp(s^{(\varepsilon_{N})})
does not intersect the boundary
\partial I
. Then, we define the
local one-particle Hilbert spaces
as:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{N}\cap I)_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

where the inclusion results from extension by zero. The
twisted-local fermion and Majorana algebras
are, thus, defined as:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}_{\CAR}(\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I),C),

which are considered to be subalgebras of
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

As an immediate consequence of this definition we have the following properties:

Proposition 3.6
.

The local one-particle Hilbert spaces and twisted-local fermion and Majorana algebras form inductive systems with respect to the wavelet renormalization group. Specifically, we have:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{N}\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I).

Moreover, we recover the (twisted) quasi-local structure of
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)\subset\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)\subset\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I^{\prime}),

\displaystyle I\subset I^{\prime},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I),\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I^{\prime})]=\{0\},

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I),\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I^{\prime})]=\{0\},

\displaystyle I\cap I^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\overline{\bigcup_{I\subset S^{1}_{L}}\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\overline{\bigcup_{I\subset S^{1}_{L}}\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)},

where
[\!\ ,\!\ ]
in the second line is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded or twisted commutator with respect to the grading given by the parity operator
(-1)^{F}

[
8
]
.

3.1.2
A decay estimate

We provide decay estimate on the finite products ,
\prod_{n=1}^{M}m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+n}k)
, that directly implies (
102
) in Sobolev-type norms for compactly supported Daubechies scaling functions by dominated convergence. The proof adapts a multi-scale decomposition strategy in
[
20
]
used to obtain regularity estimates for such scaling functions to finite products. A similar albeit less general statement can be found in a recent article by one of the authors
[
69
]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\phi=\!\ _{K}\phi
be a compactly supported Daubechies scaling function with
K\geq 2
, i.e.:

{}_{K}m_{0}(l)

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{1+e^{-il}}{2}\right)^{K}{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(l).

(106)

where
{}_{K}\mathcal{L}
is a certain trigonometric polynomial. Then, for
j\in\mathbb{N}
, and
j^{-1}M\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\left|\chi_{[-2^{M}\pi,2^{M}\pi)}(l)\!\prod_{n=1}^{M}\!{}_{K}m_{0}(2^{-n}l)\right|

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi_{[-2^{M}\pi,2^{M}\pi)}(l)\max\{e^{C}\!,\!\pi^{-\mathcal{K}_{j}}\}\!\!\left(\tfrac{|\sin(\frac{1}{2}l)|}{|l|}\right)^{\!K}\!(1+|l|)^{\mathcal{K}_{j}},

(107)

where
\mathcal{K}_{j}=j^{-1}\log_{2}(q_{j})
,
q_{j}=\sup_{l\in\mathbb{R}}\prod_{n=0}^{j-1}{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(2^{-n}l)
, and
|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(l)|\leq 1+C|l|
.

Proof.

Clearly,
{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(0)=1
and, thus,
|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(l)|\leq 1+C|l|
. Put,
{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(l)\!=\!\prod_{n=0}^{j-1}{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(2^{-n}l)
such that
\prod_{m=0}^{j^{-1}M-1}{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)=\prod_{n=1}^{M}{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(2^{-n}l)
and
q_{j}=\sup_{l\in\mathbb{R}}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(l)|
. Now:

1.

Assume
|l|\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\prod_{m=0}^{j^{-1}M-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-mj+1}l)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{C(1-2^{-m})|l|}\leq e^{C}.

2.

Assume
2^{j(M^{\prime}-1)}<|l|\leq 2^{jM^{\prime}}
for some
M^{\prime}\leq j^{-1}M
:

\displaystyle\prod_{m=0}^{j^{-1}M-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)|

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{M^{\prime}-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)|\prod_{n=M^{\prime}}^{j^{-1}M-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(nj+1)}l)|

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{M^{\prime}-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)|\prod_{n=0}^{j^{-1}M-M^{\prime}-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(nj+1)}\underbrace{2^{-jM^{\prime}}l}_{|\!\ \cdot\!\ |\leq 1})|

\displaystyle\leq e^{C}q_{j}^{M^{\prime}}=e^{C}2^{jM^{\prime}\mathcal{K}_{j}}\leq e^{C}(1+|l|)^{\mathcal{K}_{j}}.

3.

Assume
2^{M}<|l|\leq 2^{M}\pi
:

\displaystyle\prod_{m=0}^{j^{-1}M-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)|

\displaystyle\leq q_{j}^{j^{-1}M}=2^{M\mathcal{K}_{j}}\leq\pi^{-\mathcal{K}_{j}}(1+|l|)^{\mathcal{K}_{j}}.

Thus, we have
\prod_{n=1}^{M}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}(2^{-n}l)|=\prod_{m=0}^{j^{-1}M-1}|{}_{K}\mathcal{L}_{j}(2^{-(mj+1)}l)|\leq\max\{e^{C},\pi^{-\mathcal{K}_{j}}\}(1+|l|)^{\mathcal{K}_{j}}
for
|l|\leq 2^{M}\pi
. In combination with the formula
\prod_{n=1}^{M}\Big|\tfrac{1-e^{-i2^{-n}l}}{2}\Big|=\prod_{n=1}^{M}|\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}2^{-n}l)|=\Big|\tfrac{2\sin(\frac{1}{2}l)}{2^{M+1}\sin(2^{-(M+1)l})}\Big|
the result follows.

\Box

It is known that
\mathcal{K}_{2}\leq(K-1)(2-\tfrac{3}{4}\log_{2}(3)

[
20
]
implying:

\displaystyle-K+\mathcal{K}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq-(1+(K-1)\underbrace{(\tfrac{3}{4}\log_{2}(3)-1)}_{\sim 0.1887})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle K\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}-\infty.

(108)

Thus, the decay in (
107
) can be made arbitrarily fast in a polynomial sense by increasing
K
, and we note that
K_{j_{2}}\leq\mathcal{K}_{j_{1}}+C\tfrac{j_{1}}{j_{2}}
for
j_{1}<j_{2}

For convenience, we state the following regularity estimate for the scaling function from
[
20
, Lemma 7.1.2]
:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\phi=\!\ _{K}\phi
be a compactly supported Daubechies scaling function with
K\geq 2
, then it satisfies the following decay estimate:

\displaystyle|\hat{s}(l)|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(1+|l|)^{-K+\mathcal{K}}

(109)

for
\mathcal{K}=\inf_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{K}_{j}
. which implies
s\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
if
\mathcal{K}<K-(1+\alpha)
.

3.2
Momentum-cutoff renormalization group

A second renormalization group that we make heavy use of in Section
4
implements a sharp cutoff in momentum space
[
69
]
:

Definition 3.9

(Momentum-cutoff renormalization group)
.

Let
\chi_{\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}
be the characteristic function of
\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\subset\Gamma_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\subset\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, and let:

\displaystyle R^{N}_{N+1}(\hat{\xi})_{k}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(k)(\hat{\xi}_{\per})_{k},

\displaystyle R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\xi})_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(k)(\hat{\xi}_{\per})_{k},

(110)

for
\xi=(\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)})\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. The
momentum-cutoff renormalization group
is densely defined by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{N+1}(\hat{a}(\hat{\xi}))

\displaystyle=\hat{a}(R^{N}_{N+1}(\hat{\xi})),

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}(\hat{\xi}))

\displaystyle=\hat{a}(R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\xi})).

(111)

The momentum-cutoff renormalization group enjoys the same properties as the wavelet renormalization group, cp. Proposition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The one-particle map of the momentum-cutoff renormalization group has the following properties:
R^{N}_{N+1}:\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{N+1,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is

1.

well-defined,

2.

C
-compatible,

3.

isometric, i.e.
R^{N}_{N+1}{}^{\!\!*}\circ R^{N}_{N+1}=\1_{\mathfrak{h}_{N}}
,

4.

asymptotically compatible, i.e.
R^{N+1}_{\infty}\circ R^{N}_{N+1}=R^{N}_{\infty}
.

Moreover, the inductive-limit Hilbert space agrees with that of the wavelet renormalization group,
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\cong L^{2}(S^{1}_{L})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and
R^{N}_{\infty}{}^{*}\circ R^{N}_{\infty}=\1_{\mathfrak{h}_{N}}
.

Let us also state the real-space form of the asymptotic one-particle maps of the momentum-cutoff renormalization group:

\displaystyle R^{N}_{\infty}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\xi_{x}\!\ e^{i\frac{\pi}{2L}(\!\ .\!\ -x)}\tfrac{\sin(\pi\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}(\!\ .\!\ -x))}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{2L}(\!\ .\!\ -x)},

(112)

for
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. This makes it evident that images,
\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(a(\xi))
, of lattice-localized localized operators,
a(\xi)
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(I)
, are not strictly localized in
I\subset S^{1}_{L}
, in contrast with the wavelet scaling maps.

3.3
The scaling limit of lattice vacua

We are now in a position to determine the scaling limit of the family of lattice vacua
\{\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
according to (
89
). Using either the wavelet or the momentum-cutoff renormalization group, we find:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{M,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\langle R^{N}_{M}(\xi),(1-S^{(N+M)}_{0})R^{N}_{M}(\eta)\rangle_{N+M}

(113)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N+M}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N+M,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\langle R^{N}_{M}(\hat{\xi})_{k},P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N+M}}(k)R^{N}_{M}(\hat{\eta})_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},

such that the scaling-limit states
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, is well-defined if we impose the
renormalization condition
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\lambda_{N}

\displaystyle=m,

(114)

for some
m\geq 0
(the
physical mass
of the continuum fermion):

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\langle R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\xi})_{k},P^{(+)}_{m}R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\eta})_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}},

(115)

where
P_{m}(k)=(2\omega_{m}(k))^{-1}(\omega_{m}(k)\mathds{1}_{2}+n_{m}(k)\cdot\sigma)
with
\omega_{m}(k)=(m^{2}+k^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
n_{m}(k)=ke_{y}+me_{z}
.
If we use the Majorana mass term (
68
) instead of the Dirac mass term,
P^{(+)}_{m}
instead takes the form:

\displaystyle P^{(+)}_{m}(k)=(2\omega_{m}(k))^{-1}(\omega_{m}(k)\mathds{1}_{2}+n_{m}(k)\cdot\sigma),

(116)

with
n_{m}(k)=ke_{y}+me_{x}
, which satisfies:
CP^{(+)}_{m}=(\mathds{1}-P^{(+)}_{m})C
for all
m\geq 0
. In this case, the scaling limit is also well-defined as a state on
\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(\Psi(\xi)\Psi(\eta)^{*})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\langle R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\xi})_{k},P^{(+)}_{m}R^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{\eta})_{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}.

(117)

The one-particle operator
P^{(+)}_{0}
of the massless scaling-limit state is directly related to the chiral projections
p_{\pm}
, cp. (
61
):

\displaystyle P^{(+)}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathds{1}_{2}+\sign(k)\sigma_{y})=p_{\sign(k)},

(118)

which results in the following expression of the scaling limit on the chiral algebra
\mathfrak{A}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
(and again similar for
\mathfrak{B}^{(\pm)}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
),

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi)\psi_{\pm}^{\dagger}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\tfrac{1}{2}(1\pm\sign(k))\overline{R^{N}_{\infty}(\xi)_{k}}R^{N}_{\infty}(\eta)_{k},

(119)

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi)\psi_{\mp}^{\dagger}(\eta))

\displaystyle=0,

such that the chiral parts decouple. As expected, we observe that the the scaling limit of the massless lattice splits into independent components relative to the chiral decomposition (
61
), and (
119
) is related to the projection, cf. (
72
),

\displaystyle P^{+}

\displaystyle:L^{2}(S^{1}_{L})\longrightarrow H^{2}(S^{1}_{L}),

(120)

onto Hardy space and its complement
P^{-}=1-P^{+}
. Inverting the Fourier transform in (
119
), we obtain a real-space expression that is seen to approximate the standard infinite-volume two-point function in the limit
L\rightarrow\infty

[
1
]
:

\displaystyle\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi)\psi_{\pm}^{\dagger}(\zeta))

\displaystyle=\mp\int_{S^{1}_{L}}dx\!\ \int_{S^{1}_{L}}dx’\!\ (2L)^{-1}\tfrac{\overline{\xi\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x)}\zeta\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x’)}{e^{i\frac{\pi}{L}(x-x’\pm i0^{+})}-1}

(121)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\pm\tfrac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\!\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}dx’\!\ \frac{\overline{\xi\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x)}\zeta\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(x’)}{x-x’\pm i0^{+}}.

Now that we have established sensible candidates, (
115
) & (
117
), for the scaling limits of the families of lattice vacua, we establish the convergence to these limits for the wavelet renormalization group using the results of Section
3.1.2
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\{\omega^{(N)}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a family of quasi-free lattice states, each defined by an operator
0\leq S^{(N)}\leq 1
on
\mathfrak{h}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
(
CS^{(N)}=(1-S^{(N)})C
in the self-dual case). Assume that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the kernel of
S^{(N)}
is diagonal in momentum space and converges point-wise to the kernel of an operator
0\leq S\leq 1
(
CS=(1-S)C
),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}S^{(N)}(k)_{ij}

\displaystyle=S(k)_{ij},

\displaystyle k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},i,j=1,2,

(122)

where
\langle e_{k,i},S^{(N)}e_{l,j}\rangle_{N}=2L_{N}\delta_{k,l}S^{(N)}(k)_{ij}
and
\langle e_{k,i},Se_{l,j}\rangle_{\infty}=2L\delta_{k,l}S(k)_{ij}
. Then, the renormalization group flow (
89
) of
\omega^{(N)}
converges to
\omega^{(N)}_{S}=\omega_{S}\circ\alpha^{N}_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\|\omega^{(N)}_{M}-\omega^{(N)}_{S}\|

\displaystyle=0.

(123)

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the statement for the two-point functions because the states are quasi-free and the algebras
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
are generated (in norm) the by algebraic span of annihilation and creation operators respectively the Majorana operators. We state the proof only for the complex fermions
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
as it is completely analogous for the Majorana fermions
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the properties of the renormalization group we have:

\displaystyle|(\omega^{(N)}_{M}-\omega^{(N)}_{S})(a(\xi)a^{{\dagger}}(\eta))

\displaystyle=\langle R^{N}_{\infty}(\xi),(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}-SR^{N}_{\infty})(\eta)\rangle_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi\|_{N}\|(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}-SR^{N}_{\infty})(\eta)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi\|_{N}\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{j=1,2}|\hat{\eta}^{(j)}_{l}|\|(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}\!-\!SR^{N}_{\infty})(e_{l,j})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi\|_{N}\|\eta\|_{N}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{j=1,2}\!\!\|(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}\!-\!SR^{N}_{\infty})(e_{l,j})\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big)^{\!\frac{1}{2}}.

Next, we evaluate the last factor in the last line explicitly:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{j=1,2}\!\!\|(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}\!-\!SR^{N}_{\infty})(e_{l,j})\|_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{j=1,2}\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i=1,2}\!\!|\langle e_{k,i},(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}\!-\!SR^{N}_{\infty})(e_{l,j})\rangle_{\infty}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}|\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}k)|^{2}|(S^{(N+M)}_{\per})(k)_{ij}-S(k)_{ij}|^{2}\delta_{0,\frac{\pi}{L}(k-l)\!\!\!\mod 2L_{N}},

where the last line follows from the scaling equation in momentum space (
102
), the inner products,

\displaystyle\langle e_{k,i},R^{N}_{M}(e_{l,j})\rangle_{M}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}2^{\frac{M-N}{2}}\Big(\prod_{m=1}^{M-N}m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+m}k)\Big)\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{-i(k-l)x}

(124)

\displaystyle=2L_{N}\delta_{ij}2^{\frac{M-N}{2}}\Big(\prod_{m=1}^{M-N}m_{0}(\varepsilon_{N+m}k)\Big)\delta_{0,\frac{L}{\pi}(k-l)\!\!\!\!\mod 2L_{N}},

\displaystyle\langle e_{k,i},R^{N}_{\infty}(e_{l,j})\rangle_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}k)\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{-i(k-l)x}

\displaystyle=2L_{N}\delta_{ij}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{s}(\varepsilon_{N}k)\delta_{0,\frac{L}{\pi}(k-l)\!\!\!\!\mod 2L_{N}},

for all
k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, and the periodic extension of
S^{(N+M)}(k)_{ij}
to
k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

As a consequence of the decay estimate on
\hat{s}
in Lemma
3.8
, Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, and the fact that the states are quasi-free (
7
), we know that
\omega^{(N)}
converges to
\omega^{(N)}_{S}
in the weak
∗
sense. Since the algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is finite dimensional, we know that this is equivalent to strong convergence, hence the result follows.

\Box

Corollary 3.12
.

Assuming the renormalization condition (
114
), the renormalization group flow of the family of lattice vacua
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
converges to
\omega^{(N)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
(in norm) at any scale
N
.

Proof.

The renormalization condition implies the point-wise convergence of the kernel
S^{(N)}_{0}(k)=\1-P^{(+)}_{\lambda_{N}}(k)\rightarrow\1-P^{(+)}_{m}
for
N\rightarrow\infty
.

\Box

Remark 3.13
.

The statement of Lemma
3.11
is remains valid if we replace the wavelet renormalization group by the momentum-cutoff renormalization group. To see, this we observe that:

\displaystyle|\langle e_{k,i},(R^{N+M}_{\infty}\!S^{(N+M)}\!R^{N}_{N+M}\!-\!SR^{N}_{\infty})(e_{l,j})\rangle_{\infty}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2L_{N}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{k,l}|(S^{(N+M)})(l)_{ij}-S(l)_{ij}|^{2},

for all
k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
because:

\displaystyle\langle e_{k,i},R^{N}_{M}(e_{l,j})\rangle_{M}

\displaystyle=2L_{N}\delta_{ij}2^{\frac{1}{2}(M-N)}\delta_{k,l},

(125)

\displaystyle\langle e_{k,i},R^{N}_{\infty}(e_{l,j})\rangle_{\infty}

\displaystyle=2L_{N}\delta_{ij}\varepsilon_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{k,l}.

4
Approximation of conformal symmetries

It follows from (
119
) that the chiral subalgebras of the fermion and Majorana algebras,
\mathfrak{A}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, together with the scaling limit
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\omega^{(\infty)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
are conformal field theories with central charge
c=1
respectively
c=\tfrac{1}{2}

[
21
,
26
]
. But, we would like to understand how the
\Diff_{+}(S^{1}_{L})
-covariance arises in its differential form, i.e. the
Virasoro algebra
, in the scaling limit from the lattice data,
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, and
\omega^{(N)}_{0,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.
To this end, we combine the method of operator-algebraic renormalization and its specific realizations introduced in Section
3
with the well-known results on Bogoliubov transformations and their implementability collected in Section
2.1.1
to analyze the approximation of conformal symmetries by the so-called
Koo-Saleur formula

[
60
]
, see also
[
68
,
96
]
.

4.1
The Koo-Saleur approximants

To motivate the Koo-Saleur formula for the lattice analogues of the Virasoro generators, we first introduce some terminology, following
[
68
]
. The discussion stays rather formal and assumes that we are given a conformal field theory defined on the circle
S^{1}_{L}
with Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle H=\int_{S^{1}_{L}}dx\,h(x),

(126)

acting on some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, where
h
is the Hamiltonian density (a local quantum field on
\mathcal{H}
). The Hamiltonian density is itself given by the chiral and anti-chiral energy-momentum tensors,
T
and
\overline{T}
, via

\displaystyle h(x)=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\big(T(x)+\overline{T}(x)\big).

(127)

The Fourier modes of the energy-momentum tensors,

\displaystyle L_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{2L}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{S^{1}_{L}}\!dx\,e^{ikx}T(x)\!+\!\tfrac{c}{24}\delta_{k,0},

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{k}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{2L}{(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{S^{1}_{L}}\!dx\,e^{-ikx}\overline{T}(x)\!+\!\tfrac{c}{24}\delta_{k,0},

(128)

for
k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
, form a representation of the Virasoro algebra with
central charge

c

[
21
]
,

\displaystyle[L_{k},L_{k’}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)L_{k+k’}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{c}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}-1),

(129)

\displaystyle [\overline{L}_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)\overline{L}_{k+k’}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{c}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}-1),

\displaystyle[L_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}]

\displaystyle=0,

on
\mathcal{H}
. Thus, the Fourier modes
H_{k}
of the Hamiltonian density
h
correspond to linear combinations of the Virasoro generators:

\displaystyle H_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}\int_{S^{1}_{L}}\!dx\,e^{ikx}h(x)=L_{k}+\overline{L}_{-k}-\tfrac{c}{12}\delta_{k,0},

(130)

with
H_{0}=\frac{L}{\pi}H
. In addition to the Virasoro generators, it is useful to consider their smeared versions,

\displaystyle L(X)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}}\hat{X}_{k}L_{k},

\displaystyle\overline{L}(X)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}}\hat{X}_{k}\overline{L}_{k},

(131)

for a sufficiently regular differential loop
X\in\mathfrak{l}^{\alpha}(S^{1}_{L},\mathbb{C})
(cf. Definition
3.4
and below, see, for example,
[
41
,
15
]
precise definitions of smeared Virasoro generators).

Now, the Koo-Saleur proposal is to introduce, by analogy, the lattice Fourier modes of a lattice Hamiltonian, for example,
H^{(N)}_{0}
:

\displaystyle H_{k}^{(N)}=\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{ikx}h^{(N)}_{x},

(132)

where
h^{(N)}:\Lambda_{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
is a suitable lattice Hamiltonian density. Here, we slightly abuse our notation for the sake of simplicity and denote
H^{(N)}_{k=0}=\tfrac{L}{\pi}H^{(N)}_{0}
by
H^{(N)}_{0}
as well. This leads to the following lattice analogues of
L_{k}
and
\overline{L}_{k}
:

Definition 4.1

(Koo-Saleur approximants)
.

The
Koo-Saleur (KS) approximants
of a lattice Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}
with density
h^{(N)}:\Lambda_{N}\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
or
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
are given by
12
12

12

The prefactor
\sim k^{-1}
used in
[
68
]
has no intrinsic meaning on the lattice at finite scale and is replaced by
\tfrac{\pi}{2L_{N}}|\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)|^{-1}\sim\tfrac{\pi}{L}k^{-1}
for small
\varepsilon_{N}
which also avoids Fermion doublers at the boundary of the Brillouin zone.
:

\displaystyle L_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{k}^{(N)}+\tfrac{\pi\varepsilon_{N}}{2L\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}\Big[H_{k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big]\Big),

(133)

\displaystyle \overline{L}_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{-k}^{(N)}+\tfrac{\pi\varepsilon_{N}}{2L\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}\Big[H_{-k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big]\Big),

for
k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
. Given a differential loop
X\in\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{N},\mathbb{C})
, we define the smeared KS approximants by:

\displaystyle L^{(N)}(X)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}}\hat{X}_{k}L_{k}^{(N)},

\displaystyle\overline{L}^{(N)}(X)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}}\hat{X}_{k}\overline{L}_{k}^{(N)}.

(134)

An alternative formula for the Koo-Saleur approximants is obtained by replacing
2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)
with the dispersion relation
\omega_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)
of the free massless lattice Dirac Hamiltonian
H^{(N)}_{0}
because
\tfrac{\pi}{2L_{N}}|\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)|^{-1}=\tfrac{\pi}{L_{N}}\omega_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)^{-1}
:

\displaystyle(1\!+\!\sign(k))L_{k}^{(N)}\!+\!(1\!-\!\sign(k))\overline{L}_{-k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=H_{k}^{(N)}+\tfrac{\pi}{L_{N}\omega_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)}\Big[H_{k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big],

(135)

\displaystyle(1\!+\!\sign(k))L_{-k}^{(N)}\!+\!(1\!-\!\sign(k))\overline{L}_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=H_{-k}^{(N)}-\tfrac{\pi}{L_{N}\omega_{\lambda_{N}=0}(k)}\Big[H_{-k}^{(N)}\!,H_{0}^{(N)}\Big],

In the following we show that the (smeared) KS approximants,
L^{(N)}(X)
and
\overline{L}^{(N)}(X)
, associated with the free massless lattice Dirac Hamiltonian (
44
) converge to the (smeared) Virasoro generators,
L(S^{N}_{\infty}(X))
and
\overline{L}(S^{N}_{\infty}(X))
in a sense we make precise below.

As a direct consequence, we find that the (unitary
13
13

13

Note that
L^{(N)}_{k}{}^{*}=L^{(N)}_{-k}
and
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}{}^{*}=\overline{L}^{(N)}_{-k}
.
) exponentials,

\displaystyle U^{(N)}(X)

\displaystyle=\exp(iL^{(N)}(X)),

\displaystyle\overline{U}^{(N)}(X)

\displaystyle=\exp(iL^{(N)}(X)),

(136)

as well as the (automorphic) dynamics,

\displaystyle\sigma^{(N)}_{tX}

\displaystyle=\Ad_{U^{(N)}(tX)},

\displaystyle\overline{\sigma}^{(N)}_{tX}

\displaystyle=\Ad_{\overline{U}^{(N)}(tX)},

(137)

for
X\in\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{N},\mathbb{R})
, converge to implementers of localized diffeomorphisms in
\Diff_{+}(S^{1}_{L})
and their automorphic action on
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
respectively
\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

It is important to note that we have two degrees of freedom in the correspondence between lattice and continuum models: it may be necessary to shift the ground-state energy and scale the energy axis in order to match the two in the scaling limit. That is, we should allow for hamiltonian the following adjustment of the lattice Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H^{(N)}

\displaystyle\mapsto a_{N}H^{(N)}+b_{N}\mathds{1},

(138)

where
a_{N},b_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
are model-dependent constants.

4.2
The case of massless free lattice fermions

We determine the KS approximants for the complex fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
of the free massless (
\lambda_{N}=0
) Dirac Hamiltonian (
44
) according to Definition
4.1
using the following lattice Hamiltonian density
14
14

14

Note that the expression for
h^{(N)}_{x}
is chosen to be symmetric with respect to the basic lattice translation
x\mapsto x+\varepsilon_{N}
. A different choice, for example, directly using the summand in (
44
) corresponds to partial integration in the scaling limit and, thus, a modification by a total divergence.
:

\displaystyle h^{(N)}_{x}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{N}^{-2}\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(2)}_{x}-a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(2)}_{x}+a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x-\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(1)}_{x}-a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{x}a^{(1)}_{x}+\textup{h.c.}\Big)\in\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}},

\displaystyle x\in\Lambda_{N}

(139)

Although we evaluate (
133
) only for
\lambda_{N}=0
, we note that (
71
)) shows that the lattice approximation effectively generates a contribution that behaves like Majorana mass at finite scales. Similar to (
45
) and the Fourier modes of the lattice Hamiltonian density are given by additive second quantization:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}dF_{0}(h^{(N)}_{k})=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l+k}h^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l},

(140)

\displaystyle h^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\hskip-28.45274pt0&\hskip-28.45274pte^{-i\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1\\
e^{i\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

Now, because of (
18
), the KS approximants are also given by additive second quantization:

\displaystyle L_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}dF_{0}\Big(h_{k}^{(N)}+(2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k))^{-1}\Big[h_{k}^{(N)}\!,h^{(N)}_{0}\Big]\Big),

(141)

\displaystyle \overline{L}_{k}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{L}{\pi}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}dF_{0}\Big(h_{-k}^{(N)}+(2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k))^{-1}\Big[h_{-k}^{(N)}\!,h^{(N)}_{0}\Big]\Big),

Explicitly, we find for the KS approximants of the complex fermion algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{k})=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\!\!\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l+k}\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l},

(142)

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hskip-28.45274pt-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2}))&\hskip-28.45274pte^{-i\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1\\
e^{i\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2}))\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k})=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\!\!\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l-k}\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l},

\displaystyle\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hskip-28.45274pt\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2}))&\hskip-28.45274pte^{-i\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1\\
e^{i\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})}\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)-1&\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2}))\end{pmatrix}.

We find the explicit expressions for the KS approximants in terms of the chiral decomposition (
61
) by applying the chiral projectors
p_{\pm}
to the one-particle operators
\ell^{(N)}_{k}
,
\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hskip-7.11317pt-\sin(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})\!)&\hskip-7.11317pt-i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}+\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l+k)\!)^{2})\\[7.11317pt]
i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}+\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l+k)\!)^{2})&-\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\end{pmatrix},

(143)

\displaystyle\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hskip-7.11317pt\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})\!)&\hskip-7.11317pti(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}+\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l-k)\!)^{2})\\[7.11317pt]
-i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}+\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l-k)\!)^{2})&\sin(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\end{pmatrix}.

(144)

From the latter expressions we can read of the KS approximants of the chiral and anti-chiral subalgebras (
62
), i.e.
p_{-}\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)p_{-}=\ell^{(N)}_{-,k}
respectively
p_{+}\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)p_{+}=\ell^{(N)}_{+,k}
:

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{-,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{-,k})=\tfrac{-1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2}))\hat{\psi}_{-|l+k}^{{\dagger}}\hat{\psi}_{-|l},

(145)

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{+,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{+,k})=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2}))\hat{\psi}_{+|l-k}^{{\dagger}}\hat{\psi}_{+|l}.

A direct computation verifies the validity of the lattice analogue of the Virasoro algebra (
129
) at
c=0
for
L^{(N)}_{k}
and
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}
at first order in
\varepsilon_{N}
, with the following explicit expressions for the chiral and anti-chiral subalgebras:

\displaystyle\left[L^{(N)}_{-,k},L^{(N)}_{-,k’}\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L_{N}}{\pi}2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(k-k’))\frac{\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k’)^{2}}{\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}(k+k’))^{2}}\left(L^{(N)}_{-,k+k’}+R^{(N)}_{-,k+k’}\right)

(146)

\displaystyle \left[L^{(N)}_{+,k},L^{(N)}_{+,k’}\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L_{N}}{\pi}2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(k-k’))\frac{\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k’)^{2}}{\cos(\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}(k+k’))^{2}}\left(L^{(N)}_{+,k+k’}+R^{(N)}_{+,k+k’}\right),

where the remainders
R^{(N)}_{k+k’,-}
,
R^{(N)}_{k+k’,+}
are in
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{N}^{2})
and given by:

\displaystyle R^{(N)}_{-,k+k’}

\displaystyle\!=\!\!\tfrac{-1}{\pi}\!\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}(k\!+\!k’)\!)^{2}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\tfrac{k+k’}{2}\!)\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\tfrac{k+k’}{2}\!)\hat{\psi}_{-|l+k+k’}^{{\dagger}}\hat{\psi}_{-|l},

(147)

\displaystyle R^{(N)}_{+,k+k’}

\displaystyle\!=\!\!\tfrac{1}{\pi}\!\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}(k\!+\!k’)\!)^{2}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\tfrac{k+k’}{2}\!)\!)\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\tfrac{k+k’}{2}\!)\!)\hat{\psi}_{+|l-(k+k’)}^{{\dagger}}\hat{\psi}_{+|l},

A similar computation starting from (
75
) yields the KS approximants for the two copies of the Majorana algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
making up
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
, which we also denote by
L^{(N)}_{k}
and
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}
by a slight abuse of notation:

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{k})=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}=\pm}\!\!\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma|-(l+k)}\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma^{\prime}|l},

(148)

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}}{2}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!k)\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\!-\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2}))(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)&\\[-2.84544pt]
&\!\!\!\dots\\[-2.84544pt]
-i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}\!+\!\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!k)\!)^{2})(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)&\end{matrix}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{matrix}&i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}\!+\!\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!k)\!)^{2})(1\!+\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\\[-2.84544pt]
\dots&\\[-2.84544pt]
&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!k)\!)(1\!+\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\!-\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\end{matrix}\right),

\displaystyle\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k})=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}=\pm}\!\!\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma|-(l-k)}\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\hat{\Psi}_{\sigma^{\prime}|l},

(149)

\displaystyle\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}}{2}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!k)\!)(1\!+\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\!+\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)&\\[-2.84544pt]
&\!\!\!\dots\\[-2.84544pt]
-i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}\!+\!\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!k)\!)^{2})(1\!+\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)&\end{matrix}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\begin{matrix}&i(\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}l)^{2}\!+\!\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!k)\!)^{2})(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\\[-2.84544pt]
\dots&\\[-2.84544pt]
&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!k)\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!)\!+\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\end{matrix}\right),

with identical expressions for
\tilde{\Psi}_{\pm}
. The analogues of the restrictions (
145
) of
L^{(N)}_{k}
and
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}
to the chiral and anti-chiral subalgebras are (in agreement with (
145
)):

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{-,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{-,k})

(150)

\displaystyle=\!\tfrac{-1}{4\pi}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\!\big(\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!k)\!)\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt+\!\tfrac{1}{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!+\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!\big)\!\hat{\Psi}_{-|-(l+k)}\hat{\Psi}_{-|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-1}{4\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l+\tfrac{k}{2}))\hat{\Psi}_{-|-(l+k)}\hat{\Psi}_{-|l},

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{+,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{+,k})

\displaystyle=\!\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\!\!\!\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!\!\!\big(\!\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!k)\!)\cos(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt+\!\tfrac{1}{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)(1\!-\!\cos(\varepsilon_{N}(l\!-\!\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\cos(\varepsilon_{N}\tfrac{k}{2})\!)\!\big)\hat{\Psi}_{+|-(l-k)}\hat{\Psi}_{+|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\cos(\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{N}k)^{2}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}(l-\tfrac{k}{2}))\hat{\Psi}_{+|-(l-k)}\hat{\Psi}_{+|l},

where we used the identity,

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\tfrac{1}{2}(f_{\pm}(l,k)+f_{\pm}(-(l\pm k),k))\hat{\Psi}_{\pm|-(l\mp k)}\hat{\Psi}_{\pm|l}

\displaystyle=L_{N}\delta_{k,0}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}f_{\pm}(l,0),

(151)

which is a consequence of the self-dual CAR, to arrive at each of the last lines.

4.2.1
Relation with Temperley-Lieb algebras

In the original work of Koo-Saleur
[
60
]
an essential step consists in relating the Hamiltonian lattice density
h^{(N)}_{x}
to generators
e_{x}
of a Temperley-Lieb algebra
\TL_{\delta}
with loop parameter
\delta
. Let us briefly explain how such a relation with Temperley-Lieb algebras
[
88
,
44
]
arises in our setting, specifically in the case
c=\tfrac{1}{2}
which is intimately connected with the transverse-field Ising model
[
79
]
.
Invoking the self-dual formulation involving the Majorana mass term in Section
2.6
, we can rewrite the Hamilton (
71
) in terms of a pair of anti-commuting complex fermions
c^{(1)}
,
c^{(2)}
in momentum space,

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\theta(k)\hat{\psi}_{+|k}+\theta(-k)\hat{\psi}^{{\dagger}}_{-|-k},

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\theta(k)\hat{\psi}_{+|-k}+\theta(-k)\hat{\psi}^{{\dagger}}_{-|k}

(152)

which yields:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-},>0}}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\dagger}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)&\!\!\!\!-i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})\\
i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})&-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)\end{pmatrix}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}

(153)

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\!\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-},>0}}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\dagger}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)&\!\!\!\!-i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})\\
i((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})&\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)\end{pmatrix}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}.

Here, we restricted ourselves to anti-periodic boundary conditions (
-
) for simplicity. Next, we apply additional Bogoliubov transformations,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{x}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=i\sigma_{z}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma_{x}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{c}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}

(154)

to arrive at two copies of the Hamiltonian of transverse-field Ising model with mixed-sector boundary conditions
[
80
]
:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-},>0}}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\dagger}h^{(N)}_{\TIM}(k)\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(1)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(1)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}+\!\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-},>0}}\!\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix}^{\!\!\dagger}h^{(N)}_{\TIM}(k)\begin{pmatrix}\hat{z}^{(2)}_{k}\\
\hat{z}^{(2)\!\ {\dagger}}_{-k}\end{pmatrix},

(155)

where
h^{(N)}_{\TIM}(k)=-\sin(\varepsilon_{N}k)\sigma_{y}+((\cos(\varepsilon_{N}k)-1)+\lambda_{N})\sigma_{z}
. Now, we are in a position to explicitly spell out the relation with two commuting‘ copies of the Temperley-Lieb algebra
\TL_{\delta=\sqrt{2}}
(on
2^{N+2}
strands). This is achieved by introducing the Majorana fermions on the refined lattice
\Lambda_{N+1}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{(j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=z^{(j)}_{x}+z^{(j)\!\ {\dagger}}_{x},

\displaystyle\psi^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}

\displaystyle=(-i)(z^{(j)}_{x}-z^{(j)\!\ {\dagger}}_{x}),

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=1,2,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{N},

(156)

and defining the Temperley-Lieb generators:

\displaystyle e^{(j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+i\psi^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}\psi^{(j)}_{x}),

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=1,2,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{N+1}.

(157)

This leads to:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{0}

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{i}{4}\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\big((1-\lambda_{N})\psi^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}\psi^{(j)}_{x}+\psi^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N}}\psi^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}\big)

(158)

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\big((1-\lambda_{N})e^{(j)}_{x}+e^{(j)}_{x+\varepsilon_{N+1}}\big)-(2-\lambda_{N})2^{N}\varepsilon_{N+1}^{-1}

Therefore, the KS approximants at criticality (
\lambda_{N}=0
) can be equivalently computed using the Temperley-Lieb generators
e^{(j)}_{x}
(see
[
84
]
for further explanations concerning this perspective in the setting of OAR).

4.2.2
A modification of the Koo-Saleur formula

In Section
4.2.4
, we prove the convergence of the KS approximants in representations with non-trivial central charge
c\neq 0
(see Lemma
4.10
). But, this requires a certain modification of the original formula by Koo and Saleur to avoid spurious contributions due to the periodic boundary conditions introduced by the Fourier transform for one-particle functions on the dual momentum-space lattice
\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. While this modification has a concise form in terms of one-particle operators, it is also possible to formulate it in terms of the Hamiltonian density (
139
), and, moreover, in terms of Hamiltonians expressed via Temperley-Lieb generators as in Section
4.2.1
.

In the formalism of second quantization, the Fourier transform of the Hamiltonian density is given by a one-particle operator
h^{(N)}_{k}
according to formula (
140
). The modification of the Koo-Saleur formula is then given by:

\displaystyle h^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}h^{(N)}_{k}(l)\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(l+k)e_{l+k}\otimes\overline{e}_{l}

(159)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{l^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}e^{il^{\prime}x}\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(l^{\prime}+k)\Big)\Big(\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}h^{(N)}_{k}(l)e^{-ilx}e_{l+k}\otimes\overline{e}_{l}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}(x)\underbrace{\Big(\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}h^{(N)}_{k}(l)e_{l+k}\otimes\overline{\tau^{(N)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}|-x}e_{l}}\Big)}_{=h^{(N)}_{k}(x)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}h^{(N)}_{k}\Big)(0),

where
\overline{e}_{l}
is short hand for
\langle e_{l},\!\ \cdot\!\ \rangle_{N}
for all
l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
. The characteristic function
\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}
is understood to remove additional contributions arising from periodic boundary conditions on
\Gamma_{N}
, i.e.,
h^{(N)}_{k}
is not understood to be
\tfrac{2\pi}{\varepsilon_{N}}
-periodic in
k\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
. This formula together with (
141
) implies that the modified one-particle KS approximants resulting from the modified
h^{(N)}_{k}
can be written as:

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Big(\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}\ell^{(N)}_{k}\Big)(0)

(160)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}(x)\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}^{-1}\Big(h_{k}^{(N)}(x)+(2\sin(\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k))^{-1}\Big[h_{k}^{(N)}(x),h^{(N)}_{0}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L_{N}}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(l+k)e_{l+k}\otimes\overline{e}_{l},

with the obvious definition of
\ell^{(N)}_{k}(x)
. An analogous formula holds for
\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}
. At the level of the second-quantized operators, we obtain the following modifications:

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Big(\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}H^{(N)}_{k}\Big)(0),

(161)

\displaystyle H^{(N)}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{2\pi}\epsilon_{N}^{-1}\!\!\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{N}}\!\!\!e^{iky}\Big(a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y+\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(2)}_{y-x}\!-\!a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y}a^{(2)}_{y-x}\!+\!a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y}a^{(2)}_{y-\varepsilon_{N}-x}\!-\!a^{(1)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y}a^{(2)}_{y-x}

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97733pt\!+\!a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y-\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(1)}_{y-x}\!-\!a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y}a^{(1)}_{y-x}\!+\!a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y-\varepsilon_{N}}a^{(1)}_{y-x}\!-\!a^{(2)\!\!\ \dagger}_{y}a^{(1)}_{y+\varepsilon_{N}-x}\Big),

and

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Big(\overline{\widehat{\chi_{\Gamma_{N}}(\cdot+k)}}\ast_{\Lambda_{N}}L^{(N)}_{k}\Big)(0),

(162)

\displaystyle L^{(N)}_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{k}^{(N)}(x)+\tfrac{\pi\varepsilon_{N}}{2L\sin(\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{N}k)}\Big[H_{k}^{(N)}(x),H_{0}^{(N)}\Big]\Big),

and analogously for
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}
. It follows from (
161
) and Section
4.2.1
that
H^{(N)}_{k}(x)
and, thus,
L^{(N)}_{k}(x)
,
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}(x)
can be expressed in terms of Temperley-Lieb generators and the action of the braid group (realized in terms of the Kauffman bracket or Jones representation, cf.
[
80
,
84
]
).

It is evident from these formulas that we recover the original Koo-Saleur formulas for
N\rightarrow\infty
because
\chi_{\Gamma_{\infty}}\equiv 1
, and
H^{(N)}_{k}(0)
agrees with the unmodified
H^{(N)}_{k}
(and similarly all the other modified quantities).

4.2.3
The formal scaling limit

Before we turn to the analysis of the convergence of the KS approximants in the scaling limit in the sense of Definition
3.1
, we study their formal limit in terms of the asymptotic renormalization group elements
\alpha^{N}_{\infty}
. This allows us to determine candidates for limit operators which should correspond to representatives of the Virasoro algebra (
129
).

Complex fermions
\mathfrak{A}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

Let us consider the complex fermion case first. As
L^{(N)}_{k}
,
\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}
have convenient expression in momentum space, we first observe that:

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}^{(j)}_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{ikx}\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(a^{(j)}_{x})=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{N}}e^{ikx}a^{(j)}(s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{x}).

(163)

Thus, because
\alpha^{N}_{\infty}
is
*
-morphism, we find:

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{\infty}\left(L^{(N)}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l+k})\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l})

(164)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\varepsilon_{N}^{2}\!\!\!\!\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\!\!\!\!a^{(i){\dagger}}(\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{x})\!\!\left(\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!e^{i(l+k)x}\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}e^{-ily}\!\!\right)\!\!a^{(j)}(\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{y}),

\displaystyle\alpha^{N}_{\infty}\left(\overline{L}^{(N)}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\varepsilon_{N}}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l-k})\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\varepsilon_{N}^{2}\!\!\!\!\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\!\!\!\!a^{(i){\dagger}}(\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{x})\!\!\left(\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\!\!e^{i(l-k)x}\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)_{ij}e^{-ily}\!\!\right)\!\!a^{(j)}(\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{y}).

Now, we can formally take the scaling limit
N\rightarrow\infty
using
\varepsilon_{N}^{2}\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{N}}\rightarrow\int_{S^{1}_{L}\times S^{1}_{L}}\!dx\!\ dy
and
\varepsilon_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}s^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{x}\rightarrow\delta_{x}
(in the sense of distributions), as well as,

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ell_{k}(l):=-(l+\tfrac{k}{2})p_{-},

\displaystyle\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{\ell}_{k}(l):=(l-\tfrac{k}{2})p_{+}.

(165)

The latter can equally be expressed as a formal convergence of operators on the one-particle space
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle:=R^{N}_{\infty}\ell^{(N)}_{k}R^{N\!\ *}_{\infty}\rightarrow\ell_{k},

\displaystyle\tilde{\overline{\ell}}^{(N)}_{k}

\displaystyle:=R^{N}_{\infty}\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}R^{N\!\ *}_{\infty}\rightarrow\overline{\ell}_{k}.

(166)

Therefore, the formal scaling limit of the KS approximants is:

\displaystyle L_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell_{k}),

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\overline{\ell}_{k})

(167)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l+k}\ell_{k}(l)_{ij}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\hat{a}^{(i){\dagger}}_{l-k}\overline{\ell}_{k}(l)_{ij}\hat{a}^{(j)}_{l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})\hat{\psi}^{{\dagger}}_{-|l+k}\hat{\psi}_{-|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})\hat{\psi}^{{\dagger}}_{+|l-k}\hat{\psi}_{+|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell_{-,k})=L_{-,k},

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell_{+,k})=L_{+,k},

where we invoked the chiral decomposition (
62
) in the last line. We give a precise definition of
\ell_{\pm,k}
as an unbounded operator on one-particle
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
in the next subsection. The definition of its (normal-ordered) second quantization as unbounded operator on Fock space
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}})
is consequence of the results provided in Section
2.1.1
, see especially
[
14
]
. Similarly, a formal computation of the commutators in the scaling limit, again exploiting that
\alpha^{N}_{\infty}
is a
*
-morphism, yields:

\displaystyle\left[L_{k},L_{k’}\right]

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)L_{k+k’},

\displaystyle\left[\overline{L}_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}\right]

\displaystyle\!=\!\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k’)\overline{L}_{k+k’},

\displaystyle\left[L_{k},\overline{L}_{k’}\right]

\displaystyle\!=\!0.

(168)

Thus, we formally recover a representation of the Virasoro algebra (
129
) (with central charge
c=0
) in the scaling limit of complex lattice fermions. The vanishing of the central charge is expected because the computation pertains to
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
itself respectively to the Fock representation of
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
with vacuum
\Omega_{0}
, i.e. the Schwinger cocycle (
27
) vanishes:
c_{S=0}=0
.

It is also possible to recover representations of the Virasoro algebra with non-vanishing central charge (
c\neq 0
) in the formal scaling limit by passing to the GNS representations of
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
given by the scaling limits
\omega^{(\infty)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}=\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
of the lattice vacua (
115
) & (
119
). But, at this point, we are forced to distinguish between (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}
)- and (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}
)-boundary conditions as the scaling limit
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
is a pure state while
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
is not because of the degeneracies at zero momentum.

Let us first discuss the Neveu-Schwarz sector ((
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}
)-boundary condition). According to (
118
), the scaling limit
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
is a pure quasi-free state on
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,-}
determined by the Hardy projection
P^{+}
and its complement
P^{-}=1-P^{+}
given in (
72
) via
S=P^{-}\oplus P^{+}
. Moreover, by (
119
)
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
is compatible with the factorization,
\mathfrak{A}_{\infty,-}\cong\mathfrak{A}^{(+)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathfrak{A}^{(-)}_{\infty,-}
, in to chiral components. By (
9
) the GNS representation
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
of the restrictions of
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}=\omega^{(+)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}\otimes\omega^{(-)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
can be realized on Fock space,
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}})\cong\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(+)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}})\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(-)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}})
, as:

\displaystyle\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\psi_{\pm}(P^{\pm}\xi)+\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\pm}(JP^{\mp}\xi),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}

(169)

where we choose the conjugation
J\hat{\xi}=\overline{\hat{\xi}}
which satisfies
JP^{\pm}=P^{\pm}J
.

By analogy with (
164
), we compute the formal limit of
\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\tilde{\ell}^{(N)}_{\pm,k})=\!\ :\!\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\alpha^{N}_{\infty}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{0}(\ell^{(N)}_{\pm,k})))
: resulting in:

\displaystyle\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\tilde{\ell}^{(N)}_{\pm,k})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\ell_{\pm,k})=:L_{\pm,k},

(170)

\displaystyle [L_{\pm,k},L_{\pm,k^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k^{\prime})L_{\pm,k+k^{\prime}}+\big(\tfrac{L}{\pi}\big)^{2}c_{P^{\mp}}(\ell_{\pm,k},\ell_{\pm,k^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k^{\prime})L_{\pm,k+k^{\prime}}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{1}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}-1),

where we used the fact that
\ell_{\pm,k}
has off-diagonal parts in the Hilbert-Schmidt class. This shows that we formally obtain a representation of the Virasoro algebra with central charge
c=1
as expected, and the GNS vector of
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
has conformal weight
h=0
, i.e.
L_{\pm,0}\Omega_{0}=0
.

In the Ramond sector ((
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}
)-boundary condition), we purify
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
by doubling according to (
11
). The doubled projections, taking the role of
P^{\pm}
in the Neveu-Schwarz sector, are explicitly given by,

\displaystyle P_{S_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\sum_{k\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\theta(\pm k)&0\\
0&\theta(\mp k)\end{pmatrix}e_{k}\otimes\langle e_{k},\!\ \cdot\!\ \rangle_{\infty}+\tfrac{1}{2L}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}&\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\\
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}&\tfrac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}e_{0}\otimes\langle e_{0},\!\ \cdot\!\ \rangle_{\infty},

(171)

on
\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
. The GNS representation
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
of
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}=\omega^{(+)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}\otimes\omega^{(-)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
is then realized on
\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}})\cong\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(+)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{\textup{a}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(-)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}})^{\otimes 2}
by:

\displaystyle\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}(\psi_{\pm}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\psi_{\pm}(P_{S_{\pm}}(\xi\oplus 0))+\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\pm}((J\oplus J)P_{S_{\mp}}(\xi\oplus 0)),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h}^{(\pm)}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}},

(172)

where
\psi_{\pm}
on the right hand side is the obvious two-component extension.

Computing the formal limit as in (
170
) of the KS approximants with respect to
\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
leads to:

\displaystyle\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}(\tilde{\ell}^{(N)}_{\pm,k})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}(\ell_{\pm,k})=:L_{\pm,k},

(173)

\displaystyle [L_{\pm,k},L_{\pm,k^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k^{\prime})L_{\pm,k+k^{\prime}}+\big(\tfrac{L}{\pi}\big)^{2}c_{P_{S_{\mp}}}(\ell_{\pm,k},\ell_{\pm,k^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}(k-k^{\prime})L_{\pm,k+k^{\prime}}+\delta_{k+k’,0}\tfrac{1}{12}(\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)((\tfrac{L}{\pi}k)^{2}+2).

We recover the Virasoro algebra (
129
) with
c=1
by redefining the limit at zero momentum,

\displaystyle L_{\pm,0}

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dF_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}(\ell_{\pm,k})+\tfrac{1}{8},

(174)

which shows that the GNS vector of
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
has conformal weight
h=\tfrac{1}{8}
, i.e.
L_{\pm,0}\Omega_{0}\otimes\Omega_{0}=\tfrac{1}{8}\Omega_{0}\otimes\Omega_{0}
.

Majorana fermions
\mathfrak{B}_{N,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
.

The computation of the formal limit of the KS approximants in the Majorana case is structurally identical, hinging only on the formal convergence (
166
) of the one-particle operators (
148
) & (
149
):

\displaystyle\ell^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ell_{k}(l):=-(l+\tfrac{k}{2})\tfrac{1}{2}(\1-\sigma_{z}),

\displaystyle\overline{\ell}^{(N)}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{\ell}_{k}(l):=(l-\tfrac{k}{2})\tfrac{1}{2}(\1+\sigma_{z}).

(175)

Therefore, we only state the final results pertaining to
\mathfrak{B}_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}
itself,

\displaystyle L_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell_{k}),

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\overline{\ell}_{k})

(176)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{-1}{4\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}(l+\tfrac{k}{2})\hat{\Psi}_{-|-(l+k)}\hat{\Psi}_{-|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\sum_{l\in\Gamma_{\infty,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm}}}(l-\tfrac{k}{2})\hat{\Psi}_{+|-(l-k)}\hat{\Psi}_{+|l}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell_{-,k})=L_{-,k},

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{0}(\ell_{+,k})=L_{+,k},

and the scaling limits of the lattice vacua
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
,

\displaystyle L_{\pm,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\ell_{\pm,k}),

(177)

and
\omega_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
,

\displaystyle L_{\pm,k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{L}{\pi}dQ_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}(\ell_{\pm,k})+\tfrac{1}{16}\delta_{k,0},

(178)

both of which have
c=\tfrac{1}{2}
and conformal weight
h=0
respectively
h=\tfrac{1}{16}
. The normal-ordered quantizations
dQ_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+}}
and
dQ_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}
are defined with respect to the self-dual analogues of (
169
):

\displaystyle\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-}}(\Psi_{\pm}(\xi))

\displaystyle=\psi_{\pm}(P^{\pm}\xi)+\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\pm}(CP^{\mp}\xi),

\displaystyle\xi
